| **F-93** | **A run could not name its own tool calls, and the missing join invited a plausible substitute that produced wrong answers three times in one day** | **FIXED** (migration `20260826150000` + `loop.server.ts`). `tool_calls.trace_id` is the only key it shares with `agent_runs`, and **`agent_runs` had no such column** — `driver.server.ts:2713` states it: the ids *"are carried down from each `runAgentLoop` result rather than looked up"*, because looking them up was impossible. **The link between a run and what it actually DID existed only in memory, for the life of that run.** Measured: `tool_calls.trace_id` matches **0 of 1,689** `agent_runs.id`, and no `%trace%` table exists anywhere. **The cost, all on 2026-08-26:** three investigations nearly concluded wrongly. Twice a workspace filter was used as a stand-in and returned a **different track's crew** — once almost producing the finding *"the release agent makes zero tool calls"* when it makes seven; S4 hit the same wall verifying F-87 and correctly stopped rather than infer. **A join that does not exist is worse than a slow one, because it invites a plausible substitute.** It also blocked a specified feature: S2's collision derivation is defined as *"newest `tool_calls` row per ACTIVE `agent_run`"*, unjoinable until now. Fixed by recording the SAME `traceId` already stamped on every `tool_calls` row — one column, one line, `traceId` was already in scope. **NOT BACKFILLABLE and the migration says so:** the correlation for the 1,689 existing runs was never written down, so they stay NULL forever, **and NULL means "unknowable", never "made no calls"** — F-76's distinction at a new table, where getting it backwards turns a missing join into a false claim about what an agent did. No DEFAULT, deliberately: a default would fabricate a correlation for every historical run. |
| **F-94** | **`github.readFile` has 35 production calls and is catalogued nowhere, so every consequence lookup for it fail-closes** | **OPEN, small, and found by a test I wrote wrongly.** Building the collision derivation I asserted that two readers on one file are not contested, using `repo.read` and `github.readFile` — and it failed. **The code was right and my test was wrong:** `grep -rn "github.readFile" src/lib` returns **nothing**. It is not in `registry.server.ts`, not in `READ_ONLY_TOOLS`, not in `CONSEQUENCES`. So `isSideEffectingTool` fail-closes it to `true`, which is `tool-consequences.ts`'s documented and correct behaviour — *"nothing is written down about what this changes"* is a statement about our knowledge, not about the tool. **The consequence is real though:** a call that only reads a file is treated as consequential everywhere consequence is consulted — approval axis defaults, and now the collision mark, which will report a shared read of it as contested. **The fix is to catalogue it** (it belongs beside `repo.read` in the read set), not to loosen the default. **The wider point, which is this session's pattern again:** `READ_ONLY_TOOLS` describes itself as *"a hand-kept copy of a fact that lives somewhere else"*, and a hand-kept copy of 30-odd names is exactly the shape that drifts — the same failure as F-80's importer count and F-90's inverted flag. A guard that diffs the mirror against the registry's own `category: "read"` would make it mechanical, and is the durable fix. I did not write that guard this pass because my first attempt at parsing the registry gave 32 where `grep -c` gave 35, and **a guard built on a parser I cannot trust would be worse than none** — it would fail on drift that is not there and be believed. |
| **F-95** | **A handback that wrote `deployments.status = 'success'` would manufacture the exact proof R-27 gates the production deploy on** | **AVOIDED BY DESIGN, and recorded so nobody "fixes" it later.** Building `submitStationByHand` (gaps #6 and #12) meant writing a `deployments` row from a URL a person pasted. **Every one of the 42 rows in that table is `status = 'success'`, so the wrong constant was one keystroke away** — and `release.publish` requires exactly such a row, which is F-36's standing finding that the loop cannot produce one. Writing `success` from a pasted link would have turned **a person's unverified claim into the evidence R-27 exists to demand**, and it would have looked like a feature while doing it. It writes **`status: 'claimed'`** and **`triggered_by: 'handback'`** (the column otherwise carries only machine triggers: `ci-poll-tick | promote | release`), so it **cannot** satisfy `release.publish`. **That is the design, not a shortfall:** F-36's never-once-written `deployment` member row finally gets written, and the proof does not. Same rule one table over — a handed-back PR is `pr_open`, **never `merged`**, because we were told a pull request exists and nobody checked whether it landed, and `merged` is what promotion reads. **The press is recorded FIRST**, before any write can fail: R-18 forbids counting a run a person touched as unattended, F-79 caught a false acceptance that survived precisely because a human act left no trace, and if the writes fail the record still says somebody reached in — the safe direction to be wrong in. **Only Build and Ship accept a link**; the other five stations produce a spec, a decision or a prototype, and accepting a URL there would file an empty artifact where real work belongs. |
| **F-96** | **I wrote F-76's defect back into the codebase, in the same commit as the test that forbids it** | **FIXED** (this commit), and worth more than the fix. `submitStationByHand` inserted `workspace_id` and `user_id` into `spine_track_members`. That table has five columns — `track_id, artifact_kind, artifact_id, station, created_at` — and holds neither. **The `as never` cast Supabase inserts need in this repo defeated the typechecker**, so `bunx tsc --noEmit` returned 0 on a statement PostgREST would refuse at runtime, and the insert had no error check, so the refusal would have been **silent**: the `deployments` row written, the member row dropped, the station still reading empty and nothing anywhere saying why. That is F-76's shape exactly — a failed query wearing the face of empty work — committed by the session that spent the morning fixing F-76, alongside a test file arguing against inventing columns. **The generalisable part is not "check the schema".** It is that `as never` converts a compile-time error into a runtime one, so every table reached through it is unprotected by the tool that protects the rest of the codebase, and needs its column list written down somewhere a test can read. The first guard I wrote for it was itself wrong — it parsed keys out of the insert with a regex that captured `id` from the type annotation `(dep as { id: string })` and missed `station` because shorthand has no colon — so it was replaced with a precise negative that asserts the two phantom columns are absent. **A guard that fails on drift that is not there gets believed, which is the same reason the `READ_ONLY_TOOLS` drift guard was declined an hour earlier.** |
| **F-97** | **The acceptance query in `CLAUDE.md` promised 0 and had been returning 1 for a day** | **FIXED** (this commit). Every session loads `CLAUDE.md`, and it gave `entry_station='sense' AND station='learn' AND waived='[]'` as the measured test with the answer **0**. Measured 2026-08-26 it returns **1** — `d1168015`, which completed 2026-08-25 16:28 UTC. The acceptance is still not met, for a reason the query cannot see: a person answered a boundary call mid-run, so R-18 disqualifies it (F-79). **The danger is the direction of the error.** A stale 0 does not read as stale — it reads as a test that is still failing honestly, so the first session to run it and get 1 has every reason to announce the acceptance met, and the paragraph explaining why it is not sits in a different finding. The same block's `is_sample` form has drifted 1 → 2 for the same reason (F-61 updated). **The fix is not a new number, which would go stale on the next completed track.** `CLAUDE.md` now carries the query that returns 0 *because the disqualification is in the SQL* — a `NOT IN` over approvals with `decided_at IS NOT NULL`, joined to tracks through `agent_runs.mission_id`. F-79 stops being a sentence someone has to remember and becomes a join. Measured the same day: honest query 1, of those human-decided 1, non-sweep-driven 0, **true acceptance 0**. |
| **F-98** | **The sense graveyard was never the recoverable work. Six tracks on a REAL workspace were, and they are F-76 casualties sitting at exactly the two stations F-76 broke** | **RELEASING, 2 of 6 pressed 2026-08-26 15:55 UTC.** F-92 spent its effort on 24 tracks parked at `sense`, and every one of them is on an `is_sample = true` workspace that `track-tick` excludes by id — unreachable by the sweep, so the release it proposed was a no-op that would have read as *"the fix did not work"* rather than *"nothing was ever driven"*. That is F-76's shape a third time: an action that fails silently and gets misread as evidence about something else. **The work that can actually move is much smaller and much better.** On sweep-drivable workspaces there are only **7 open tracks**, and **6 are held `station-cannot-finish` — 3 at `decide`, 3 at `design`.** Those are precisely the two stations F-76 broke: decide asked `decisions.forecast_text` (it is `forecast_claim`), design asked kind `design_memory` (design files `prototype`). `decideCorrection` sets `station-cannot-finish` when a station *"has what it needs on the record and still finished empty"*, which is exactly what a 400 read as `data ?? []` produces. **All six last moved between 2026-08-23 and 2026-08-25 05:41 UTC; F-76's fix landed 2026-08-26 09:17 UTC** (`e13c24b5f`, verified an ancestor of the deployed `5fc47c486`, so the sweep now runs the corrected check). **All six entered at `sense`,** so unlike the fixture tracks they are acceptance-eligible in principle. **The cost of releasing them, stated plainly:** a `press` row is a human act, so R-18 disqualifies a pressed track from ever being THE acceptance run. They are spent as proof that the fix works end to end on real work, not as candidates. **CORRECTION, same day:** this row first named `c6c26412` a clean candidate *"never pressed"*. It is not. Its own drive log opens `press@13:09`, at creation, so my own true-acceptance query disqualifies it and always did. I checked the ledger's other claims and not that one, which is the failure this ledger exists to prevent. **There is no clean candidate**: of 7 open tracks on sweep-drivable workspaces, 3 are pressed and the remaining 4 are all terminally held (F-99). Two released first rather than six, because a fix that works on two is evidence and a fix that works on none costs six tracks of spend to learn the same thing. |
| **F-99** | **The spine bounds a station twice and only one ceiling has a door, so the control called "unstick" cannot un-stick anything** | **FIXED** (this commit), and it is the best answer yet to *why 73 tracks and zero `sense` → `learn`*. `MAX_STATION_ATTEMPTS = 3` bounds a station that FILES NOTHING. `MAX_STATION_DRIVES = 12` (F-43) bounds a station DISPATCHED forever without converging — the case `attempts` cannot see, because `out-of-time` deliberately costs no attempt. Both are right. **`retryStation` reset `attempts` and left `station_drives` untouched**, so a released track cleared its hold, was driven once, tripped F-43 and re-held as `going-in-circles` — **terminal** — inside one tick. **Measured on the live database 2026-08-26:** two tracks released by hand at 40 and 29 drives both re-held within ten minutes, and **every open track on a sweep-drivable workspace was already past the ceiling — 81, 63, 53, 42, 40, 29.** So the documented way to recover work could not recover any of it. **It failed in the worst direction:** the hold cleared, the board showed the work moving, and it was terminal again before anyone looked twice — a button whose visible effect is real and whose actual effect is nothing. **Found by spending it rather than by reading it:** the release was run as a two-track probe precisely so a wrong hypothesis would cost two tracks and not six, and the probe returned `going-in-circles` at zero tokens, which is what made the second ceiling visible. **The fix is a split, not a reset.** A PERSON's release (`retryStation`, `submitStationByHand`) now clears `station_drives`; the automatic escalation-resume in `driver.server.ts` deliberately does NOT, because a machine path that clears its own ceiling has none, and F-43 measured that at 776 runs and roughly $2.70 across six tracks with not one station advanced. A test pins the presence AND the absence, and was mutation-tested in both directions. **The cost is stated rather than buried:** those two tracks re-held for free, and after this they will dispatch a crew and spend real money. That is a person choosing to spend, which is the only kind this should buy. **PROVEN ON REAL WORK, 2026-08-26 16:10 UTC.** Track `8391835f` (design) was released at 16:08 with `station_drives = 0`. The 16:10 sweep drove it, it did **not** trip `going-in-circles`, `station_drives` went 0 → 1, and **it filed a new `prototype` at 16:10:47 — its first artifact in 34 hours.** It now holds `out-of-time`, which is resumable and costs no attempt (F-14), so it drives again next tick. The same track carried 27 artifacts already, including 2 `prototype` rows at design — **exactly the kind F-76's broken check could not see, because it asked for `design_memory`.** So the two fixes are one story: F-76 blinded the self-check, the track was sent back and back until it crossed F-43's ceiling, and F-99 was the reason nothing could bring it home. |
| **F-100** | **A real observation with an unsupported root cause: the "single blocker" credential was writing signals the whole time** | **FALSE as a root cause, 2026-08-26 — and the observation underneath it is TRUE, which is what makes it worth writing down.** A concurrent session filed `docs/operations/blocker-supabase-credentials.md` concluding that a missing `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` is *"the single blocker"* stopping every track at Discover, and asked for the credential to be supplied. **The observation is real and I confirmed it:** 27 runs on `sense`-entry tracks returned `completed_with_failures` and **12 of them mention `SERVICE_ROLE`** in their output. **Three independent measurements say it is not the cause.** (1) `signals.log` **wrote 7 signals into the same real workspace between 13:30 and 14:00**, spanning the 13:54–13:57 failure window on both sides — the function was working before, between and after the runs that said it could not run. (2) The sweep reaches the database through `supabaseAdmin`, whose factory **throws outright** when that variable is absent; the sweep ran all afternoon and filed a `prototype` at 16:10, so the key is present where the server actually runs. (3) The three affected tracks hold **`out-of-time`** — the tick deadline — not any credential hold. **What the agents produced is the F-68 shape from the other side:** one output literally reads *"Per instructions: 'a missing SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY causes silent failure...'"*, so it is **quoting a claim it was given, not reporting a thing it observed**, and no prompt in `src/lib/ai/` contains that sentence. **Why this one mattered enough to chase:** the remedy was a request to the founder for a service-role key — a credential this session is explicitly forbidden to request or use, which bypasses RLS, and which on this evidence would have fixed nothing. **A believable root cause attached to a true observation is harder to catch than a wrong observation**, because the numbers check out and nobody re-asks the "so is that why?" question. The doc keeps its analysis and carries a correction banner; the rename to a dateless filename was only to clear the doc gate. |
| **F-101** | **Build's real blocker is a GitHub 401, and the binding that points there shows one repo on screen while targeting another in code** | **OPEN — founder-actionable, and it is the next thing in the chain rather than a theory.** Track `8391835f` cleared Design at 16:10 UTC (F-99) and reached Build, where it holds `tools-refused`. **Two independent agents name the same cause at 16:30:** `builder` — *"GitHub authentication failed with a 401 error ('Bad credentials'). I cannot access the repository to read files, search code, or make changes"* — and `qa` — *"the repo.tree call failed with a 401 'Bad credentials' error"*. Unlike F-100's credential claim this one is corroborated, specific, and produced by agents that were each trying to do a different thing. **The binding it resolves through** (`connection_bindings` `944406fa`, workspace `60000000`) targets `resource_id = Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app` via GitHub **App** installation `156492672` (`Supaprod`, last verified 2026-08-25 14:25). App auth mints a token from `GITHUB_APP_ID` + `GITHUB_APP_PRIVATE_KEY` and stores no secret, so a 401 means the App cannot mint — a missing private key in the deployment, or an installation that no longer covers that repo. **From this session's GitHub identity the `Supaprod` org contains only `.github`**, and `relay-homeowner-app` is not visible; that is consistent with *private and not shared with this identity* OR *does not exist*, and the two cannot be told apart from here. **A second defect, certain and independent of the 401:** that binding's `resource_label` reads **`RohitGajaraj/helio-prism-build`** while its `resource_id` is **`Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app`**. **The screen and the record disagree** — anyone reading the connector surface is told the build targets a repo it does not target, which is the F-68 shape in the connector layer, and it will survive the 401 being fixed. |
| **F-102** | **The em dashes a user could see were never in the source, so the guard built to catch them reported clean for months** | **FIXED** (this commit), founder-reported. The convention against AI fingerprints has shipped two halves for months: `humanizeText` at the AI chokepoint, and `scripts/check-humanized.sh` scanning source. **Both were working. Both were blind to where the dashes actually were.** `humanizeText` only ever saw STREAMED model text; a tool ARGUMENT is model-written text too and passed through untouched, so `signals.log({ title })` wrote the model's raw string into a column the Sense and Discover surfaces render. **Measured 2026-08-26: 120 of 1,483 `signals.title` rows carried an em or en dash, against 0 of 113 `prds.body_md` — the split is exactly which path wrote them.** A source scanner cannot see that, because the text was never in the source. **Three fixes, one per hole.** (1) `humanizeToolArgs` at BOTH provider extractors in `runtime.server.ts`, safe to run on every argument because the rewrites touch only U+2013/U+2014 and never an ASCII hyphen, so paths, uuids, slugs and URLs survive; a test pins that property. (2) `prepareSignalRows` humanizes title and content at the single sink every signal write passes, with screening deliberately left on the ORIGINAL string so nothing can smuggle a banned phrase past `screenIngestText` inside a character this rewrites. (3) The existing data cleaned: **signals, themes, opportunities, tasks, missions, prototypes, prds, learnings and decision titles all to 0.** **106 source lines in 34 files also carried them, and most were PROMPT TEXT** (`registry.server.ts` 25, `critic.server.ts` 10) — `ICE — Impact:` and `?? "—"` sit in the model's own prompt, teaching it the style it then produces, which is the root of the loop. **THE PART WORTH REMEMBERING IS HOW NEARLY IT WAS MISSED TWICE.** My first scan reported CLEAN across 548 files because my hit-counting grep did not match the output's two-space indent; my second reported 0 because `xargs -a` is not a BSD option and silently scanned nothing. Both times the instrument answered "fine" and both times it had not looked. **A guard that cannot fail is indistinguishable from a guard that passes**, which is the same lesson as the unreliable drift guard declined earlier the same day. **Also flipped the pre-commit hook to BLOCKING** (`HUMANIZE_STRICT` now defaults to 1): it had been warn-only, exiting 0 while printing, which is how a warning becomes furniture. Flipping cost nothing because the source was clean by then. **And the strict flip immediately needed a narrowing:** it fired on four decoder lines (`.replace(/&nbsp;/g, " ")`) — code that REMOVES the entity — so an exemption was added for an entity mapped to a plain space, verified in both directions, because a strict guard that cries wolf is one somebody switches off. **The one thing deliberately NOT cleaned:** 6 `decisions.forecast_claim` rows still carry dashes. The database refused the update — *"a forecast you can edit after the outcome is a retrospective, not a forecast"* — and that refusal is correct. Cosmetic tidying does not justify rewriting the one artifact the product's whole claim rests on. |
| **F-103** | **The vocabulary canon contradicted itself on a KEEP word, and half the report of it was a misread** | **HALF FIXED, half FALSE, 2026-08-27** (S4-046). **The real half:** `positioning-locked-2026-08.md:203` listed **audit trail** under *Never* for landing surfaces, while `CLAUDE.md:9` says *"Audit trail and shared brain stay, everywhere"* and names that same file as the Full canon. So the file the canon defers to banned the word the canon protects. The cause is visible in the source: the table's Register column is struck through because the register split was retired 2026-08-11, and **the per-surface Never columns were never updated with it** — retired-split residue sitting in a live file. The warning directly above the table already said the row was wrong, in those words, and the table still said it, which is why three sessions read the table and not the warning. Struck, and `audit trail` moved to the Use column where the ruling puts it. **The false half, and it matters because acting on it would have broken working copy:** *"OPERATING-MODEL bans `autonomous` twice while positioning-locked uses it as a measured claim"* is a misread. Both bans are explicitly scoped — `:32` and `:735` each read **"in product copy"** — and `positioning-locked:269` uses the word in analytical prose about the market, which is not product copy. There is no contradiction to fix. **Recorded so the next sweep does not `fix` it**, because a session removing the word from an investor answer on the strength of a product-copy rule would be following canon into a mistake. |
| **F-104** | **Discover cleared, and a track walked two stations with a real forecast at the end of it** | **MEASURED 2026-08-26 23:11 UTC, and it is the first time this has happened on a real workspace.** Track `a30238f5` had been stuck at `sense` for a day across twelve drives, filing nothing, before dying on the F-43 ceiling twice. After the sense brief was fixed AND actually delivered (S4 caught that the first fix reached nobody), it filed a **`signal` at 23:00:45**, cleared Discover, filed a **`decision` at 23:10:38**, and now stands at `define` with **no hold**. Two stations in eleven minutes, by sweep, with no human in the run. **The decision is the part that matters.** `strategist` decided *"Do not implement address reuse until post-fix abandonment evidence"* — a decision NOT to build, which is the harder and more valuable call — and recorded a forecast that is genuinely falsifiable: claim *"Address reuse is not a material driver of checkout abandonment post-single-address fix"*, observable *"Session replays show <10% of abandonments occur AFTER the single-address screen and involve users attempting and failing to reuse a prior address, across ≥500 sessions"*, horizon **2026-10-15**. That is a forecast with a number, a source, a threshold and a date, written before the outcome was known, by an agent. **It is what the product claims to be for, and until tonight nothing in the database had ever demonstrated it end to end.** Three fixes had to land together for it: the evidence definition (a session replay is evidence), the sense brief (evidence already on the record counts), and the delivery bug that meant the second one reached no seat. **This does not meet R-18's acceptance** — the track carries earlier presses from before the fixes, so it is disqualified as the acceptance run and is not being claimed as one. What it demonstrates is that the mechanism works when the crew is told the truth about what counts. **The next clean track entering at `sense` is now the one to watch**, and `learning.record`'s new horizon predicate will refuse to grade this bet before 2026-10-15. |
| **F-105** | **The one station whose job is stopping work could not record a stop** | **FIXED** (`13c1407d4`), found by watching the track that cleared Discover rather than by reading code. The Decide brief tells the crew *"A 'no' is a decision and you file it the same way as a yes"*, and at 23:10 UTC a strategist did exactly that on `a30238f5`: *"Do not implement address reuse until post-fix abandonment evidence emerges"*, with a checkable forecast behind it. **The spine walked it on to Define anyway.** `decisions.status` offers `approved`, `pending`, `standing` and `superseded` and **nothing that means no**, so the refusal was stored `approved` and read as a go — four stations of agents about to specify, design and build the thing that had just been refused. **Decide exists to prevent spend and it could not.** `decision.record` now takes `call: "build" | "do-not-build"`, defaulting to build so no existing decision silently becomes a refusal, and writes `declined`; the override applies only to a gate `approved`, because a no-go with a provenance problem is not more trustworthy for being a no. The driver asks at Decide and waives `define`, `design`, `build` and `ship`, so the track walks **Decide → Learn**. **Waived rather than closed, deliberately:** a refusal is an answer with a bet attached, that forecast comes due 2026-10-15 with a real observable, and closing the track would throw the bet away so the loop could never learn whether the "no" was right. `reopensWhen: "outcome-contested"` brings the stations back if the verdict says otherwise. **RESIDUE, one track:** `a30238f5` was already past Decide when this landed, so the waive cannot fire for it retroactively; its decision row was corrected to `declined` but the route rewrite was refused by the tool-permission classifier and was not forced. It will walk on and build something already refused, at a cost of roughly one dollar. Every track after it is covered. |
| **F-106** | **~~Build is blocked on a GitHub credential~~ WITHDRAWN 2026-08-27: the credential works, and a workspace with no binding was falling back to a dead repo** | **OPEN, FOUNDER ACTION, and it is the only thing standing between the loop and a finished walk.** F-101 reported a 401 and pointed at a missing or revoked key. **Half of it was a type error and that half is fixed** (`6423363e8`): `github.server.ts` put `process.env.GITHUB_APP_ID`, a string, into the JWT `iss` claim, and GitHub answers *"'Issuer' claim ('iss') must be an Integer"*. Found by minting a token by hand, which is the only way to tell a malformed claim from a bad key — both are 401 and both read as "Bad credentials" downstream. **The other half is real and is now measured against the DEPLOYED environment, not a local `.env`.** Track `8391835f` was released at 00:15 UTC on code carrying the fix (`latest_commit_sha` verified), driven at 00:20, and `builder` answered: *"GitHub authentication failed with a 401 error ('Bad credentials'). I cannot access the repository to read files, search code, or make changes."* **So the App itself cannot authenticate.** The binding targets `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app` through installation `156492672` (`Supaprod`, last verified 2026-08-25 14:25). **WITHDRAWN, AND DO NOT ROTATE ANYTHING.** At 01:52 UTC track `a30238f5` opened a real pull request — `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app/pull/2` — and every GitHub call in that run succeeded: `repo.tree`, `repo.read`, `studio.stage`, `studio.commit`, `studio.pr.open`, `github.ci.read`, all `ok: true`. **The GitHub App authenticates fine.** The 401s are all against a DIFFERENT repository, `RohitGajaraj/Test-Project-Cadence`, and the difference is the binding: `a30238f5` sits on workspace `60000000`, which binds to `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app` through the `Supaprod` connection verified 2026-08-25. `8391835f` sits on workspace `0b792d52`, which **has no `github` binding at all**, so it falls back to a default pointing at a repo the App cannot reach. **The fix is a binding, not a key**, and regenerating the private key would have broken the one connection that works. My earlier 'proven against the deployed environment' claim was measured on the failing workspace only, which is the check-one-place-and-generalise error a third time in one session. **Also still true and independent of the credential** (F-101): that binding's `resource_label` reads `RohitGajaraj/helio-prism-build` while its `resource_id` is `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app`, so the connector surface names a repo the build does not use, and fixing the key will not fix that. |
| **F-107** | **F-36 is not a missing mechanism. It is the same credential, and that means the founder has ONE action rather than several** | **MEASURED 2026-08-27, and it simplifies the critical path considerably.** F-36 has stood as *"`release.publish` requires a `deployments` row with `status='success'` that the loop cannot produce"*, and `SESSION-0-CONDUCTOR.md` §1b names the preview deploy as **"the missing mechanism"** and ranks building it second of five. **It is not missing.** `captureDeploymentsCore` (`deployments.functions.ts:156`) upserts `deployments` rows on `(changeset_id, environment, commit_sha)`, and `ci-poll-tick` calls it on a schedule. It reads the customer's own GitHub deployment objects, which is the right design: the proof of a deploy comes from the thing that deployed, not from us asserting one. **What it needs is a working GitHub connection** — `resolveConnector({provider: "github"})` at :26, then `readDeployments` at :37. With the App unable to mint a token (F-106) it returns `not-connected` or fails the read, so **no row is ever captured and Ship can never satisfy its own gate.** **So one credential blocks two stations, not one.** Build cannot read the repo, and Ship cannot capture the deploy that would prove the release. Regenerating the GitHub App private key, or reconciling `GITHUB_APP_ID` with the key it is paired with, unblocks **both**, and with them the first `sense` → `learn` walk this product has ever had. **The five-runnable ranking in §1b should be re-read in that light:** probe 2 does not need building, it needs connecting, and the sandbox primitive is not on the critical path for the acceptance the way that brief assumes. |
| **F-108** | **I wrote a promise into a waiver that nothing implemented, in the commit that named that defect** | **FIXED** (this commit). F-105 made a decision-not-to-build waive Define, Design, Build and Ship with `reopensWhen: "outcome-contested"`, and the comment I wrote said the stations *"come back if the verdict later shows the refusal was wrong"*. **Nothing read `reopensWhen`.** `route.ts` says so in three places — *"Nothing reads `reopensWhen`"*, *"`applyTrigger` is the evaluator and it has no caller"*, *"a waiver is a one-way door today"* — so the promise was a claim outrunning its wiring, which is the class of defect this repo is named for catching, committed in the fix that invoked it. **Found by sweeping `src/lib/spine` and `src/lib/ai` for exported symbols with zero non-test importers**, the same sweep that found `metric-probe` and `resolveApprovalPolicy` unreachable earlier the same night: three capabilities built, tested, and connected to nothing. **Wired rather than softened**, because a refusal that can never be revisited is exactly the trap the waive was chosen to avoid: the whole reason a "no" waives instead of closing the track is that the bet stays alive for Learn to settle, and a bet whose settlement can change nothing is decorative. `reopenIfOutcomeContested` is `applyTrigger`'s first caller. It fires at Learn only, on a `missed` or `mixed` verdict against the track's own newest standing learning; a `validated` verdict changes nothing, because reopening on agreement would make the trigger meaningless. An unreadable verdict leaves the route alone, since reopening four stations on a read error spends real money on a guess, and a superseded learning cannot reopen anything. **A `never` waiver — the human's own deliberate skip — is untouched by construction**, so a machine reading an outcome never undoes a person's decision. |
| **F-109** | **A backfill destroys the evidence that would prove the write path is still open** | **STANDING RULE, from S4, 2026-08-27, and it cost us the proof once already.** The rule: **do not backfill a column until the write path that filled it is verified closed.** What happened: `agent_runs.output` was wrapped, backfilled, and read 0 dashed of 2,777. Two sessions confirmed that zero and one deleted a render-side bridge on it. **The column was clean and the path was open.** S1 asked the only question that separates them — how many rows created SINCE the fix carry one — and got 9. I then backfilled again, which rewrote the single row that proved the path was open **19 seconds after the fix**, and `agent_runs` has no `updated_at`, so nothing records that it happened. **Nobody can now prove that fix worked from data that already exists.** Three compounding traps, each of which read as good news: a column total measures the backfill and not the path; `0 dashed of 0 rows` is vacuously clean and I hit that one an hour later; and a backfill is irreversible against a table with no update stamp. **The forward anchor**, since the backwards evidence is gone: count dashed rows created strictly after `2026-08-27 00:50:31.106707+00` (S4-047). **And the durable answer, shipped rather than remembered:** a `BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE` trigger now sanitises at the database, so the question stops depending on finding every writer. |
| **F-110** | **The loop walked five stations and opened a real pull request, and it did it while I was telling the founder to rotate a working key** | **MEASURED 2026-08-27 01:52 UTC.** Track `a30238f5` — the one that could not clear Discover this morning — walked **sense → decide → define → design → build** and produced, in order: 1 signal, 2 decisions (one carrying a real forecast due 2026-10-15), 3 specs, 4 tasks, 1 prototype, 1 mission and **1 changeset, with an open pull request at `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app/pull/2`**. Every GitHub call in that run succeeded. **Five stations in under three hours, agent-driven, on a track that filed nothing at all across twelve drives before the evidence fixes landed.** **AND IT CORRECTS F-106, WHICH I FILED WRONG.** I reported the GitHub App credentials as invalid and asked the founder to regenerate the private key, on the strength of a 401 measured against one workspace. The App authenticates fine. The 401s were against a DIFFERENT repository — `RohitGajaraj/Test-Project-Cadence` — reached from workspace `0b792d52`, which **has no `github` binding at all** and falls back to a default pointing at a repo the App cannot see. Workspace `60000000` binds to `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app` and works. **The fix is a binding, not a key, and rotating the key would have broken the one connection that works.** Third instance in one session of checking one place and generalising, and the most expensive one, because the remedy was destructive. **Also closes F-101's second half:** that binding's `resource_label` read `RohitGajaraj/helio-prism-build` while its `resource_id` was `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app`, so the connector surface named a repo the build did not use. Set to match. |
| **F-111** | **An unbound workspace borrowed a repository from a deployment-wide default, and four of them have written into one repo** | **FIXED** (this commit), found by chasing the 401 that F-106 got wrong. `GITHUB_REPO` names ONE repository for the whole deployment, and `resolveGitHub` fell through to it whenever a workspace had no binding of its own. So an unbound workspace would stage, commit and open pull requests against whatever repository that variable happens to name. **MEASURED: `studio_changesets` holds rows against `RohitGajaraj/Test-Project-Cadence` from four different workspaces — `0b792d52`, `482bdbb2`, `11ea33b6`, `b90da531` — and none of them is bound to it.** Four tenants writing code into one repository through a shared default. Harmless so far because those are fixtures and that token is dead, and the path is real and what it writes is code. **The env fallback's own comment says it "keeps current behavior alive until bindings exist".** Bindings exist. It now survives only where there is no workspace to be wrong about — a script or a dev shell with no tenant context — and **a named workspace with no binding is refused and told exactly that**, with a sentence naming the fix and naming the thing the product will not do. **The direction to be wrong in is the whole argument:** refusing costs a person one trip to Connectors; the alternative writes a customer's spec into somebody else's repository. Found only because the 401 was chased to its cause instead of being reported as a credential problem, which is what I did first. |
| **F-112** | **The acceptance query could not see a press, and a track was one station from making it lie** | **FIXED** (this commit), caught by S4 before the two forms diverged rather than after. R-18 is *"no human touching it mid-run"*, and the published honest form excluded only tracks whose **approvals** a person decided (F-79). **A press is exactly that touch and leaves no approval row**, so the clause could not see it. Measured 2026-08-27 02:12 UTC: track `a30238f5` sits at **Ship, one station from Learn**, with `entry_station='sense'`, `waived='[]'`, **0 decided approvals and 7 presses**. The moment it reached Learn the published query would have returned 1 and every clause it tests would have been satisfied. That is arithmetic, not a forecast. **19 of 106 tracks carry a press**, so it was never a one-track problem. One extra `NOT IN` over `track_drives.driven_via = 'press'`, which the column already records. **`continuation` is deliberately NOT counted**: that is the driver resuming its own work, not a person, and counting it would disqualify tracks nothing human ever touched. **And the track is disqualified by its history rather than its behaviour**, which is worth keeping straight: all seven presses are at `sense`, between 17:17 and 22:27, every one of them before the Discover fix landed. After 22:27 it walked `sense` → `ship` on sweep and continuation alone. Unsticking a track that could not clear Discover was the right thing to do at the time; it simply cannot also be the run that proves nobody helped. |
| **F-113** | **Define filed a spec about Design's failure, it superseded the real ones, and Ship refused the wrong document** | **FIXED** (this commit), found by watching `a30238f5` walk to Ship rather than by reading code. Define filed three specs. Two were about the work. The third was titled *"Design failed to proceed from existing specs because they lacked concrete, implementation-grade detail"*, opening *"Design has been unable to produce working mockups for the address screen due to insufficient detail."* **That is a spec about a STATION FAILURE**, which is the loop eating its own exhaust — the defect `signals.log` refuses at Discover (*"NEVER log the absence of evidence"*) and `own-artifact-source` refuses for citations — arriving one station over where nothing was watching for it. **It cost the run its ship.** The newest spec wins, so that document superseded both real ones, and Ship read it and refused: *"the PRD is still in draft… `success_metric_count: 0`"*. **The station was right to refuse**: it was grading a document about a process problem, and a process problem will never have a success metric because it is not work. So the loop reached its last station and failed there on a document it should never have been handed. **The rule is the one Discover already has**, now said at Define and delivered to every seat: a station's difficulty is something to SAY, not something to FILE, because filing it puts a run's own trouble into the record every later station reads, and the record is what this product sells. The brief names the exact sentences that were filed — *"Design could not proceed"*, *"the existing specs lacked detail"* — because a rule that names the thing is recognisable where an abstract one is not. |
| **F-114** | **The rule that stops em dashes was imported into the prompt builder and referenced nowhere** | **FIXED** (`929a87229`). `PLAIN_PUNCTUATION_RULE` was written to stop the model producing em dashes, imported at `loop.server.ts:15`, and **used in no prompt**. So it has never reached a model. That is why dashes kept arriving in user-facing output while four separate sanitisers were built downstream to remove them — `humanizeText` at the chokepoint, `humanizeToolArgs` at the extractors, `runOutput` at seven writes, and a database trigger. Every one of those cleans up after a model that was never told. **The rule existed the whole time; the prompt never carried it.** Both system prompts now do, the fresh run and the RESUMED run, because a voice that changes when a run is picked up again is a voice nobody trusts. `PLAIN_NAMES_RULE` joins it and belongs in the prompt rather than in a sanitiser: an em dash has a known replacement, a column name does not, and only the writer knows whether `checkout_single_address` means "the single-address checkout" or "the experiment that removed the second screen" — rewriting it mechanically produces a sentence that is WRONG rather than one that is merely machine-flavoured. The test renders both prompt arrays, because the defect being fixed is precisely a rule present in the file and absent from the prompt. |
| **F-115** | **Ship refused to ship until the prediction had already come true** | **FIXED** (`b3e90b85e`). Both tracks sitting at `ship` were held this way, including `a30238f5`, the best track this product has ever run. Its `release-verifier` says why in its own words: *"the spec requires <=5% abandonment on the tablet address re-confirm screen within 7 days of full rollout, but the actual outcome is 67% tablet checkout completion, meaning ~33% abandonment, far above the target. Shipping cannot proceed until the success metric is met."* **No rollout can ever satisfy that.** The number is a claim about seven days AFTER full rollout and it is being demanded before rollout: a deadlock in one sentence, closing the last station before `learn` permanently. **The seat was not wrong, it was uninformed** — its prompt says "check it against the spec it was built from", the number IS in the spec, and nothing told it which numbers in a spec are already-true and which are predictions. Same defect as F-114 in a third costume: the forecast, this product's central object, being read by seats never told what it is. At Learn nobody read it; at Ship somebody read it and mistook it for an entry condition. **Stated in BOTH directions**, because "never refuse a forecast for being unmet" alone would let a seat ship against a prediction nobody can check, and an ungradeable forecast makes the Learn verdict impossible — so the legitimate refusal is named too, and it is about MEASURABILITY, never about the value. |
| **F-116** | **The acceptance was impossible by construction, and three columns say so** | **FIXED** (`ef1db8a4e`). R-18 asks for seven stations with no human touching it mid-run. The path from Define to Ship passes three states and **a person is the only writer of every one**: `prds.status` (no `prd.approve` tool exists; the only review→approved write is the human tray at `approvals-queue.functions.ts:1403`); `prds.design_gate_status` (**116 of 119 `pending`**; the only writer is `decideDesignGate`, behind `requireSupabaseAuth`, stamping `design_decided_by: userId`; `design_stage_enabled` is true on all 21 workspaces so `pending` really does mean nobody answered); and the success metric (117 of 119 specs carry none). Ship reads the first two and refuses. **The cause is not a missing permission**: `design-critic` is already in Design's crew doing exactly this judgement, and its filing line said *"say plainly if it is sound as it stands"* — **so a pass left no trace.** Only a change wrote anything, and `pending` meant both "nobody looked" and "the critic looked and it was fine". F-76 in a third costume. `spec-gate.ts` is the place the verdict lands: pure, modelled on `decision-gate.ts` and deliberately STRICTER, because that module's argument for auto-approving is that a decisions row is a RECEIPT and **a spec is a LEVER**. A human's `rejected` is read first and can never be overturned; nine gates each name a different next action; confidence is tested POSITIVELY so null/NaN/out-of-range all ask. Wired at `critic.evaluate`, which decides WHO can cause a clearance: no seat calls the gate, an agent can only ask for its spec to be ARGUED AGAINST, and a clearance is a consequence of surviving that. |
| **F-117** | **117 of 119 specs carried no success metric, so nothing could ever be judged** | **FIXED** (`b99071c6d`). Two holes in one chain, either alone sufficient. (1) **Nothing said to write a measure.** `prd.draft` does extract success metrics, but its prompt says *"Extract only what the text supports. Never invent a metric"*, and the text is the body it writes from `prd-writer`'s brief. That seat's `job` line always said "how anyone would know it worked"; its `file` line — the one that says what to pass, and the one the seat acts on — did not. (2) **Nothing compiled the oracles.** Every clause is written `oracle_kind: null` and `compileContractOraclesCore` fills it; it has fired since RPT-23 from `draftContractFromIntent`, the surface a PERSON drafts through, and never from `generatePrd`, the path every agent-written spec comes down. Live: 2 specs with a standing metric, 1 with all clauses classified. **The second hole is the more dangerous one now that F-116 is in**: gate 9 refuses a spec nothing can check, correctly, but without the compile it would have refused every agent-written spec **for a reason about our own plumbing rather than about the spec**, and a refusal like that reads as a judgement. |
| **F-118** | **The audit row that justified acting alone had no reader** | **FIXED** (`3c2f3a045`). `decision-gate.server.ts` states the rule the whole autonomy design rests on — *"an auto-approval nobody can audit is worse than a queue"* — and writes a `workspace_audit_log` row carrying the gate's exact sentence and every fact it acted on, under a comment saying "that row is what a human reads before overturning". **No surface has ever read it.** The only readers of that table were the two writers and a billing card looking for claim events. The argument had been made in writing, above the code that writes the row, and a rule stated in a comment is not a rule anybody can check. It goes on **Guardrails**, because that is where a person sets what the crew may do without them, and what the crew then DID without them belongs on the same page. Also fixed in the function I was already in: `getGuardrailOverview` read `rulesRes.data ?? []`, so a refused query arrived as "no rules" and the page said "Nothing checks your AI calls yet" over a table it could not read. |
| **F-119** | **A failed read of the autonomy dial granted more autonomy than anyone set** | **FIXED** (`3a0d517e8`). `loadAgentArc` destructured `const { data }` and never looked at `error`, so a refused or timed-out read arrived as `data: null`, took the absence branch, and returned **"trusted"** — the widest arc short of ambient. An operator who had deliberately pinned an agent to `observing` would have had that pin silently discarded by a transient database error. **F-76's shape on a permission dial**, which is the worst surface it could land on: elsewhere the cost is a page saying "nothing here", here it is an agent acting without the review its operator asked for. 190 of 283 live agents have no autonomy row, so the absence branch is the one most calls already take, which is why the failure branch joining it would never have been noticed. On error the answer is `observing`, which **asks rather than freezes** — one review queued, nothing stopped — and the distinction from `autonomy-policy.ts`'s "never fall to an extreme" argument is that that one is about a permanent missing config row and this is one transient read on one tool call. |
| **F-120** | **An unreadable forecast desk reported an empty one, and then vanished** | **FIXED** (`537f0bf14`). Four reads in `forecast.functions.ts` swallowed their error and returned empty, on the one desk whose purpose is that overdue calls get answered. **Worse than a wrong number:** `ForecastDeskPanel` returns null when all three of its reads come back empty — deliberate and right for a workspace with nothing to settle — so a total read failure showed no error and no zero. **The desk disappeared from the page.** The fourth read was the most misleading: `summarizeForecastCalls([])` over an unreadable table produces a real-looking rate built on no rows. **I nearly bulldozed a deliberate decision**: my first version threw on every error, and four existing tests failed with a better reason than my change — *"Migrations and deploys are two switches with no enforced order, and PostgREST answers an unknown column with an error rather than a null"*. Both concerns are right and about DIFFERENT errors: `42703`/`PGRST204` is a deployment-ordering fact and fails soft; anything else is a runtime fact and now raises. The four tests became eight, none weakened. **Also settles S4's "15 overdue forecasts":** 13 are on sample workspaces the tick correctly skips, the real backlog is 2, and both already carry a drafted verdict — they wait because `canAutoSettle` requires the linked outcome to have been settled BY A PERSON, which is deliberate and closes the chain agent-judges-outcome-then-agent-judges-forecast. **That gate is correct and was not touched.** |
| **F-121** | **The mission goal said the same sentence twice** | **FIXED** (`56dd29ef8`), found by S1 on screen. `goal: row.origin ? ${row.title}. ${row.origin} : row.title` composes the sentence, a full stop, and the sentence again whenever a track's origin IS its title, and it is stored that way in `missions.goal` rather than doubled at render time. Measured: 1 of 106 tracks has an origin character-identical to its title, 3 more begin with it, 2 of 397 missions with a goal repeat themselves. **It will grow rather than shrink** — the sentence a person types at `/start` becomes both the title and the origin. `src/lib/track-origin.ts` holds the rule, in neither lane's prefix. **The test the module exists for is the first one:** a rule written to remove duplication is one edit from removing the thing worth reading, and nobody files a bug about a good line going missing, so a real origin carrying its own fact must survive untouched and only a LITERAL prefix is trimmed. |
| **F-122** | **The dash guard's premise had drifted, so copy we type ourselves was unguarded** | **FIXED** (`7ab7eafe2`). The new check computes REACHABILITY rather than keeping a second list: start at every component and route, follow `@/` imports transitively, check what that reaches. It is exactly as narrow as reality and cannot go stale. **Four tests check the checker before it reports anything** (F-102's real lesson: two scans that day reported CLEAN over live hits), and it was mutation-tested — a dash planted in `spine/correction.ts`, which the shell guard does not scan, fails the suite by file and line. **One hit it found was mine, an hour old**: `track-origin.ts` had literal dashes inside a character class, the exact defect S1 warned me about in the same message that reported the bug that file fixes. Guarded three ways because a wrong link is worse than none: only on a track, only onto a decision with no spec yet (`.is("prd_id", null)`, so a second spec cannot steal the first one's bet), and failures are REPORTED rather than thrown, since the spec row is real by then and throwing would tell the seat its spec does not exist. After the deploy the chain is merge → preview within two minutes → `release.publish` → Ship → Learn, and every one of those steps has a row in the database proving it has run before. |

So "we could not reach it", "this repo is not ours to deploy" and "there was nothing to do" were one indistinguishable outcome. **The cost was measured the hard way an hour earlier:** this check has run every two minutes for weeks, and asked whether the one repo the loop builds into carries the marker, **nothing in the system could answer** — the repo is private to the App installation and unreadable from outside too. That is S4's mistake — made three times in one night and self-corrected each time — repeated by the session that had just been told about it, and the founder's rule arrived the same hour: a measurement of current rows may shape PRIORITY and must never shape DESIGN, because most of these rows are demo seed. Here it had shaped a *conclusion*, which is worse than a design. **WHAT SURVIVES, unchanged:** `ci-poll-tick` is active `*/2` and its code path is real (read, not inferred); the merge stopped on 2026-07-10 with 21 expired, 8 rejected, 7 pending and 0 executed since; F-75 fixes the queueing; and **the critical path is still deploying `main`**, because nothing downstream can run while every merge queues for a person who stopped answering. **WHAT IS NEW AND SHARPER:** the one real changeset that DID reach `merged` — `helio-labs/atlas-installer-portal` — produced **zero Deno rows**, so the preview step has never succeeded on real work even when handed exactly what it needs. For **22 of the 29 pending tool-call gates the run they held is over**, so approving cannot unblock anything; seven more (`memory.promote`) have no `agent_runs` row at all; and **none is past its expiry**, so nothing will ever clear them. The youngest is 33 days old. That is the founder's own bar failing on the one surface whose job is telling a person what needs them. `gatesLiveWork` on `ApprovalQueueItem` carries it, in **three states, and the null is load-bearing**: `false` means we looked and the work is over, `null` means we cannot say — no mission, no run, or the lookup failed — and collapsing those seven into `false` would say the work had finished when nothing ever started. `halted` and `waiting_approval` count as LIVE, the one easy inversion: those are exactly the states an approval exists to end. Age cannot substitute, since a 33-day-old call whose run is still queued is genuinely waiting. **The typechecker forced the best part:** making the field required meant nine other item kinds refused to compile until each declared it, and an optional field would have let a new kind default silently. |

The founder corrected the APPROACH: that fits the platform to whatever rows happen to be sitting in the database, and most of ours is demo seed. `signals.theme_id -> themes.id` is the platform truth and holds regardless. **The generalisation, which cost me a headline within the hour (F-124b): a measurement of current rows may shape PRIORITY and must never shape DESIGN.** One query, no cap — a client-side `listThemes` read stops at the 300 newest, so an older cluster would silently lose its name. The 818 are correct, not missing. |

S4 measured every real merge failure in the product's life: **eight, of which five are conflicts** — the single commonest way a real merge has failed here, and under F-75 auto-merge the loop meets it again with no person in the run. F-41 is not weakened; its premise simply does not cover this class. **The fix is to fall through, not to add machinery:** a refusal about the work takes the ordinary path, the attempt counts, and three of them hand it to `decideCorrection`, which resolves a conflict **by construction** because `studio.commit` branches off the CURRENT default-branch head. Both halves required (tool gate AND the word "conflict"), and *"CI is still running"* is deliberately absent with its own test saying why: the work is fine and the answer is to wait, so correcting it would destroy a good change over a transient state. |

This work has been graded."*) against what the query actually tests. **It changes nothing today** — both learn tracks are already `done`, and `station='learn' AND status<>'done'` returns 0 — so it is pure protection. | **F-132** | **A best-effort pass started gating a lever, and its silence blamed the spec** | **FIXED** (`dc69eaaa1`). `critic.server.ts` runs the design lens as a second model call and says so: *"Best-effort - a failed design pass never drops the base spec red-team verdict above."* Correct while the lens only augmented a receipt. It stopped being correct the moment `spec-gate.ts` gate 8 began reading a missing design verdict as **"Nobody has read this design back against the spec yet"** — a statement about the WORK — when the cause may be a second model call of OURS that did not return. `if (design) review.design = design;` left the key absent, and an absent key cannot say which of three things happened. Live: **3 of 5 rows carry the lens, 2 do not**, so it is variable rather than systematic, which is exactly the shape that gets blamed on whatever it lands on. **I wrote the warning against this in F-117 four hours earlier and then built one** — second time in a night, both found by checking my own gate against real rows. The fix is a fact: inside `target.kind === "prd"` a design read was WANTED, so a null means we asked and did not get one. Gate 8 now says *"the design read did not come back, so this is waiting on us rather than on you"* for that case. **Not a gate of its own** — three tests pin that a spec clearing on every other count still clears whether the flag is true, false or absent. |

Only seven tables carry `is_sample`; for the rest the workspace join is a proxy, and **four seeded `deployments` rows sit on a workspace whose `is_sample` is false**. Counting fixtures as real work produced a wrong headline three times from S4 and once from me in one night. Column added to `deployments`, `studio_changesets`, `spine_tracks`, `agent_runs`, `agent_approvals`, `decisions`, applied one at a time and **verified against the schema each time** — three ALTERs returned `499 request_cancelled` and two had in fact completed server-side while the client gave up, so reading `information_schema` after each is the only reason I know which; `if not exists` is what made the retries safe. **The backfill is a fact, not a fingerprint.** S4's fingerprint (sequential ids, shared milliseconds, `example.com`) is good for looking, and marking a row seeded on the strength of its id SHAPE risks hiding real work. Measured instead: ids group into seven prefixes each holding the same four suffixes — the seed copied into seven workspaces — and **every one of those 28 rows carries an `example.com` URL while no other row does**. So the test is the URL, which is a fact about what the row CLAIMS: `example.com` is RFC 2606's reserved documentation domain, cannot resolve, and a deployment pointing at it definitionally did not happen. 28 marked, 0 without evidence. **`is_sample = true` is PROVEN seeded; `false` means NOT PROVEN SEEDED, never proven real**, and that asymmetry is in the column comment. |

Neither answers the other.** The 13 Deno previews prove it. They sit on a sample workspace, so they are demo WORK — but their `deploy_url`s are real, distinct, hosted addresses with unique build hashes, written by the real `ci-poll-tick` across three days. **They are genuine deploys that actually executed.** So F-124 was RIGHT that the mechanism works and wrong to offer sample rows as proof about real work; F-124b was RIGHT that every row is on a sample workspace and wrong to let that imply the mechanism is unproven. **The honest synthesis: the preview machinery is proven, thirteen times over three days. | **F-149** | **The loop HTML-escaped every tool result before the model read it, so the builder committed corrupted source** | **FIXED 2026-08-31** (`src/lib/ai/loop.server.ts`). `loop.server.ts:2043` ran `xmlEscape(JSON.stringify(result))` and then `.slice(0, 2000)` on every tool result before it reached the model, with a second inline copy on the approval-replay path. `xmlEscape` rewrites `&`, `<` and `>`, **so a builder reading a TypeScript file back saw `=&gt;` where the file says `=>`, `&lt;div&gt;` for `<div>`, and `&amp;&amp;` for `&&`** — and `studio.stage`'s contract is *"the FULL new file text, not a diff"* (`registry.server.ts:2117`), so it re-sent what it was shown and the corruption reached the branch. **Measured on `Supaprod/relay-homeowner-app`: two open pull requests carried 41 and 22 HTML entities in `AddressStep.tsx` against 0 on main, and the loop's own repair commit is titled *"fix(test): unescape => in AddressStep.test.tsx to restore TS parse validity"*.** The self-correct path fired, spent its whole `CI_FIX_BUDGET` on each branch, and could not win, **because the damage was upstream of everything it could reach.** The `.slice` was the second half: cutting `JSON.stringify` output at a fixed offset lands mid-string and hands the model unparseable JSON. **The escape was there for a real reason** — content could otherwise forge the closing tag and break the fence — **so it was replaced, not deleted:** a per-run id on both tags makes the boundary unforgeable without touching a byte of payload, and values are shortened individually by a binary-searched per-value ceiling so the envelope stays valid JSON and a shortened value says so in words. **Guarded by `a-tool-result-must-reach-the-model-unaltered.test.ts`, which did not exist before and caught two bugs in the first implementation.** Full suite 12,980 pass / 0 fail, `tsc` 0. **This supersedes F-36 as Tier 0.1**: `studio.commit` appears 8 times in `driver.ts`, not 0, and all six chain steps are briefed, so F-36's premise is stale. |

(1) `resolveToolMode` carries a **dedicated branch** for it (`loop.server.ts:309`, SEAM-2 mission 3.6, shipped 2026-07-07 in `46254f619`) whose comment states the intent in as many words: *"the generic high-risk floor would park the autonomous fix loop at a gate every iteration; here the safety lives in the tool itself"* — a human-opened PR at the operator-gated `studio.pr.open`, the fix budget, and the review-pinned merge gate. The track: `entry_station='build'`, `station='build'`, `status='open'`, `last_hold='needs-a-waived-station'`, sense/decide/define/design all waived by policy, on **Helio Labs (`is_sample=false`, a REAL workspace)**, and `updated_at` has not moved since **2026-08-27 05:30** — four days. **And there is nothing else to drive: `status='open' AND last_hold IS NULL` on non-sample workspaces returns 0.** Every other open track on the real workspace carries a hold; the only hold-free tracks are on sample workspaces, which the sweep skips by design (F-90). **`correction.ts` PREDICTED THIS IN WRITING AND THE MEMBERSHIP TEST IS THE ONLY THING THAT WAS MISSING.** The F-43 note in that file, about a different track, says it word for word: *"every further slot it takes is one a live track does not get ... a halving of throughput rather than a freeze, and it is invisible: every one of those ticks reported ok in 500ms having done nothing."* **So the CLASS was understood and written down; what drifted was which holds belong to it** (S4-169). Anyone diffing a waived array against hold text is comparing ids to names, and it is a live argument for §12. **Cost is NOT the damage and it should not be reported as such:** 26 `agent_runs` were spawned for this track early on, but the drives now short-circuit at the hold and spend nothing — `runs_24h` is 2, and both are today's CI-fix dispatches against missions, not this track.| **NOT FIXED — and the reason to be careful is that the obvious fix is wrong.** Adding `needs-a-waived-station` to `TERMINAL_HOLDS` stops the churn and **also removes the only signal that this track exists**, which is F-152's shape one layer up and the trap SESSION-0 §1 names outright: *"a fair guard can delete the only stuck signal"*.
