It happened four days earlier, TO S0 ITSELF, and was written down where anyone would have found it.** `coordination/answers/S2/A-006-…md` §6, on `main`, written 2026-08-27 by an S0: ***"There are two sessions acting as S0. I did not write A-005 and have never ruled on this fold before now. I have NOW-S0.md conflicts against lines I did not write and 35 commits signed S0 I did not author"*** — ending with the instruction today's sessions each re-derived independently: *"Do not commit the eight modified files in your worktree under your name. They are not mine either. Until someone claims them that tree is contested."* **So this is a standing property of this machine rather than an incident, it has struck the CONDUCTOR's own worktree as well as two lanes', and the first instance was a file on `main` that went unread.** That makes it **F-162's shape applied to F-161** — a fact recorded faithfully and read by nothing — and it means **the same doubt applies to S0's own tree and to any commit signed S0.** Surfaced by S2, who found it only after going back and reading all nine of their briefed documents rather than the four they had sampled. **WIDENED 2026-08-31, SAME DAY: IT IS AT LEAST TWO LANES AND `lane/run` IS WORSE.** S1 first reported strange commits, correctly withdrew it as their own rebase, then **re-opened it on stronger evidence: their merge conflicted on BOTH `docs/lanes/NOW-S1.md` AND `docs/lanes/log/S1.md`** — files §4 assigns to a single writer, which cannot conflict unless two sessions write them. It is **not** in their worktree (only their process has `buffalo` as cwd), so **another Conductor workspace has `lane/run` checked out and pushes to the same remote branch.** **The trailer test S0 proposed below was then run against it and it works:** on `origin/lane/run`, the commits carrying **no `Claude-Session` trailer** include exactly the ones S1 flagged — `d9acc36e3` *"S1 UNIT 7 PLANNING: value audit surface"* (a unit S1 never started, and it has **no trailers at all**), the `1636 → 105` truncation of `session-handoff.md`, and **a duplicated RUN-126 pair under two SHAs** (`a841bfc77`/`f224723f1`, `f2bc4b271`/`5cd6fd0d0`) which is the rebase producing two sets. **And it has already cost real coordination:** the other session **filed a request to S0 for gap #17's four metric queries**, so S0 may receive two requests for one number from one lane. S1 has not touched #17 and will not until it is settled. **They also unstaged S0's own answer files, NOW-S0, log/S0 and the ledger from their index rather than committing S0's files under their message** — which is the right handling and is more evidence the index is shared. **THE FOUNDER'S QUESTION, verbatim from S1 because only he can answer it: which session is S1, and does the other one get its own branch?** **NOT FIXED, and the cheap half is a detection rule rather than a lock.** **Every commit from a registered fleet session carries a `Claude-Session:` trailer; the foreign one carries none.** So `git log --format='%H %B' | grep -L 'Claude-Session'` over each lane branch names unregistered writers for free, and it is a ratchet S4 can own without writing in `src/`. **The enforcement half needs the founder**, because the only real fix is that one worktree is opened by one session — a `.git/` lock or a pre-commit identity check can be worked around by the same hand that opened the second session. **Related, and it is why this matters beyond one lane:** S1 reported the same shape earlier (commits under their identity they did not author) and correctly withdrew it after finding it was their own mid-flight rebase — **so this class produces false alarms too, and the trailer test is what separates them.**| 
| **F-162**| **One coordination answer in six says the thing already existed — and NINE are named for it in the filename, so the shape was noticed nine times and counted zero times**| **OPEN. Counted by S4-171 after S0 asked them to carry the day's pattern forward, and it is larger than the four instances that prompted it: 12 of 76 files in `coordination/answers/` report that the requested thing already existed.** Nine of the twelve **say so in their own filename** — `M07-tiered-buttons-already-exist-and-are-half-adopted`, `R005-the-link-face-is-built-and-the-other-two-already-exist`, `R015-…-createWorkspace-is-built`, `R017-019-req1-already-shipped`, `RL0-005-both-already-exist-in-meridian`, `RL0-005b-the-other-four-exist-too`, `RL0-020-the-three-gate-functions-are-in-main`, `R022b-…-it-is-fixed`, and `S0-A04-three-answers-and-one-of-them-is-already-done`. **That is this repository's own defect wearing the protocol's clothes: a fact written down faithfully, nine times, and read by nothing.** The operating model already carries the rule — *"whether something already exists here is not a research question, it is a grep, and it takes thirty seconds"* — and nobody had a number for it. **One in six says it is not happening.**| **AND THERE ARE TWO FAILURE MODES, ONLY ONE OF WHICH IS LATENCY — this is the half F-156 does not explain and it is the more useful one.** Four of the twelve landed in a single day, so F-156's stale-request mechanism is real. **But S0's S4-033 instruction was issued THAT DAY, on a fresh and entirely correct measurement, and was still stale** — because the measurement was of the **database** (71 of 366 `mission_steps` skipped, right) while the fix lived in the **code** (`step-progress.ts`, mounted, rendering *"step 6 of 8, 2 skipped"* on three surfaces). **A right number about the data co-exists with a shipped fix on the surface, and checking one does not check the other.** Cross-branch visibility would not have caught it; a grep would have.| **NOT FIXED. S4's remedy is right, costs no surface, and it is accepted here as the shape to build:** a request states **what was grepped and when**, and **an instruction older than a day is re-checked before it is issued.** **And the argument for it is S0's own, turned around** — S4 refused S4-033 because they happened to read `step-progress.ts` first, and **nothing required them to**. *"A defence that depends on the diligence of the receiver is luck, not a defence"* is precisely what S0 said to S4 about the approval gate in F-152/S4-164, and it applies here against S0. **A session in a hurry files it, a builder spends a unit, and under §0.7 that is a unit no lane is allowed to spend.**| 
| **F-163**| **`clusterSignals` re-runs and mints a fresh overlapping set of themes instead of reconciling with the ones already there**| **OPEN, and it is S0's — `src/lib/discovery`.** Reported by S1 as a measurement rather than a request (they could not file it where S0 would see it: F-156). Track `425e6887` carries **33 DISTINCT themes whose names are near-duplicates of one another** — *Alert Overload Leads to Muting* · *Notification Fatigue Leads to Disengagement* · *Alert Fatigue Leading to Muted Notifications* · *Alert Volume Leads to Muting*. **Every one is a real row.** Clustering has run repeatedly and each pass minted a NEW overlapping set rather than matching what existed, so the same underlying complaint is represented four ways and counted four times.| **Verified independently by S0 at workspace scope, 2026-08-31:** the real workspace `60000000…` holds **130 themes across 88 DISTINCT clustering minutes**, first 2026-06-14, last 2026-08-31 09:20. **Exact-title dedup is useless here and that is the point — 117 of 130 titles are distinct**, because the duplicates are semantic rather than literal. A `DISTINCT title` check would report this table as almost entirely clean. **The visible cost lands on S1's surface:** RUN-127's handed-over Discover file renders **53 headings**, which is what a person is asked to read.| **NOT FIXED.** The fix is a reconciliation step, not a better prompt: before minting a theme, match against existing themes on the workspace — **the `embedding` column already exists on `themes`**, so this is a similarity read rather than a new capability. **Two traps worth naming before anyone starts.** (1) **Do not dedupe by title**; 117 of 130 are already distinct and the defect would survive untouched. (2) **Do not delete existing rows to tidy the count** — every one is real, they are cited by whatever read them, and a Discover surface that silently loses evidence is worse than one that repeats it. Merge forward, never retro-delete.| 
| **F-164**| **THE PRODUCT'S OWN FRONT DOOR DISQUALIFIES A TRACK FROM THE ACCEPTANCE 2.1 SECONDS AFTER IT IS CREATED**| **OPEN, and it is the mechanism behind three months of zero.** Submitting the composer at `/start` creates a track and **presses it in the same breath**. `TrackRun.tsx:501-509` fires `run.mutate("press")` on `autoStart`, with a comment that is honest about it — *"the person's act was submitting the composer; landing here is that press arriving"* — and `track.functions.ts:711` writes `last_driven_via: "press"`. **The acceptance query excludes any track carrying a press.** So every piece of work born through the product's own door is disqualified before it has done anything.| **Measured 2026-08-31 across every track ever driven, and the number is not marginal.** Of **20** tracks with any drive, **18 had a `press` as their FIRST drive** and only 2 had `sweep`. **The soonest press landed 2.133 seconds after the track was created.** Then, checked against the eight open `entry_station='sense'`, `waived='[]'` tracks on non-sample workspaces: **not one is clean on both acceptance forms** — four carry 5–8 non-sweep `stage_events` and four carry presses (1, 1, 3, 7). **There was no track in the database that could satisfy the acceptance, whatever the loop did.** That is a stronger statement than F-155's *"nothing is drivable"*: even a perfectly working loop had nothing eligible to walk.| **RULING: DO NOT LOOSEN THE QUERY, AND THE TEMPTATION TO IS THE TRAP.** The obvious fix — *"exclude presses that happen mid-run, not the one that starts it"* — makes the number go up without anything improving, which is how a team fools itself and is exactly what standard #7 deletes. **The query is RIGHT and it is naming a missing capability**: R-18 asks whether work can enter and complete with nobody touching it, and today **the only way work enters is a person pressing submit**. §0.6 gap #4's return edge (a missed forecast becoming a new track at Discover) and gap #13's inbound assignment (a Linear issue becoming work) are both paths where work enters with **no press at all** — so **gap #4 is not merely Tier 0.4, it is the direct unblock for the acceptance**, and that was not visible before this. **ACTED ON, 2026-08-31 12:51 UTC:** S0 created track `d2263583-cec9-4b14-8f52-4ccedd07534f` directly on the real workspace — entry `sense`, `waived='[]'`, all seven on its path, `driven_at` NULL, **no press** — which is the shape the return edge would produce. Its `origin` column says plainly that S0 made it to test the acceptance rather than dressing it as a discovered signal. **It is the first track in this product's history eligible to satisfy R-18.**| 
| **F-165**| **"Verify the deploy by comparing BYTES" was unverifiable as written, and it cost four wrong readings in one session**| **FIXED as an instrument, 2026-08-31. The instruction is right and the method everyone reaches for cannot execute it.** SESSION-0 §M says *verify by fetching the changed asset and comparing BYTES, never a deployment id* — written after publish status lied three times in one night, and correct in spirit. **But the obvious reading fails four different ways, and S0 hit all four in one session:** (1) an **md5 of the served HTML** reports CHANGED on every fetch, because the page carries a **per-request render timestamp** (`u:1788173545712`) — a false positive that would have been reported as a landed deploy; (2) **content-hashed asset filenames** cannot move for a **server-side** change, and F-152/153/154 were entirely server-side; (3) a **grep of the served bundle for a symbol** finds nothing because minification removes it — the CONTROL symbol was absent too, which is what exposed the instrument rather than the deploy; (4) **the root HTML names only 38 assets — the shell — and every lazily-mounted chunk is invisible to it**, so `collision.ts` and `boundary-ledger.ts` changed with the asset list byte-identical. **The same blind spot S4 fixed in `check:unreachable` the same hour (React.lazy, components 70 → 44, 26 mounted components reported as orphans).**| **The working instrument, measured end to end.** `supaprod.ai` is served by **Cloudflare** and returns an **`x-deployment-id`** header. Baseline recorded at 12:47Z as `b2795a4e…`; `deploy_project` fired at ~13:03Z; **the header changed to `4c94abc8…` at 13:08:39Z — 5 minutes 39 seconds later** — while the 38 root assets and the HTML byte-length stayed identical. **So the deploy landed and every byte-comparison of those assets said it had not.** Two prior beliefs are corrected by this: *"a Lovable publish takes ~20 minutes to serve"* (it took under six) and S0's own earlier reading that **`deploy_project` publishes a host nobody visits** — it does deploy production; `supaprod.lovable.app` 302s to `supaprod.ai` and the same deployment serves both.| **USE `x-deployment-id`. Record it BEFORE firing, compare it after.** It is a value the origin sets per deployment, it is downstream of any change including a server-only one, and it survives minification and lazy-chunking. **The general rule, which is the part worth carrying: a byte comparison is a proof only when the bytes you compare are downstream of what you altered** — three of the four failures above were not the deploy lying, they were an instrument that could not see the change. **Do not delete §M's warning about deployment ids:** it is about not trusting the id a publish *returns to you*, which is still right. This is the id the **origin serves**, which is a different thing and is the honest one.| 
| **F-166**| **`git cat-file -e $ref:src/...` in zsh ALWAYS says the file is present, and two lanes plus S0 read the artifact and believed it**| **FIXED as an instrument. IT FAILS BOTH WAYS, and S0 verified the mechanism first-hand rather than write down a third account from testimony — three sessions sent three different explanations and all three were incomplete.** **Which way it lies depends on the path's FIRST LETTER**, because zsh picks a different modifier: `$r:src/components/today/Board.tsx` expands to **exactly `origin/main`** — `:s` is *substitution*, the path's own slashes supply the `/from/to/` delimiters, **the path is EATEN**, and `git cat-file -e origin/main` then asks whether that REF exists, which is true for any valid ref → **FALSE PRESENT**. `$r:the-first-run/RULINGS.md` expands to **`mainhe-first-run/RULINGS.md`** — `:t` is *tail*, giving the basename `main` plus the remainder appended literally, a garbage rev → **FALSE ABSENT on a file that is there.** **AND THE CONDITION NONE OF THE THREE REPORTS CARRIED, which is why it survives testing: it only fires when the path is a LITERAL after `$var:`.** With the path in a variable — `$r:$p` — zsh parses no modifier at all, and `src/`, `the-first-run/`, `coordination/`, `e2e/` and `docs/` **all answer correctly**; S0 tested all five both ways. **So the same command is right or wrong depending on whether the path was typed or passed**, which is exactly what a person verifies once and trusts forever. In zsh, `$r:s` is the **substitution modifier**, expanded before `git` ever sees the argument. **Any path whose first character is a zsh modifier letter is destroyed, and `s` is one** — so **every path under `src/` resolves to garbage** and the `&&`/`||` chain reports *present*. Found by S1 after S2 used it to conclude `Board.tsx` was on all four refs and told S1 their contrary check was stale. **Both lanes were reading a shell artifact, and they disagreed with each other because of it.** The file was on **no** ref.| **S1's comparison table, and the last row is what makes it vicious:** `${r}:path` braced → absent (correct) · `git cat-file -s <rev>:<path>` → *fatal: path does not exist* (correct) · `git ls-tree -r --name-only <ref> \| grep -c <path>` → 0 (correct) · **`$r:docs/README.md` → present, and that one is genuinely correct** because `d` is not a modifier. **So the bug is invisible to anyone who tests it on `docs/`.** **S0 HIT IT TOO, the same day:** a loop asking `git cat-file -e $b:coordination/requests/...` returned nothing at all for four branches, which looked wrong enough to distrust — S0 switched to `comm -13` against `git ls-tree` and got the right answer for F-156. **The instrument was caught by luck, not by rigour**, which is exactly S4's argument in F-162 about defences that depend on the diligence of the receiver.| **USE `git ls-tree -r --name-only <ref>`**, or `"$ref:$path"` with the path in a **variable**, or `"${ref}:literal/path"` **braced**. Never `cat-file -e $var:literal/path`. **S4 gated it as `bun run check:branch-idiom` rather than filing a note, and that was the right call for a reason worth keeping: `docs/operations/autonomous-build-loop.md:186` is a STANDING FOUNDER RULING naming this exact command as how to verify code landed** — safe as written with a literal ref, unsafe the moment a loop puts the ref in a variable. A note cannot protect a ruling; a gate can. **AND THE SECOND FAILURE IS THE ONE TO REMEMBER, because it was caused by fixing the first.** S1 corrected the method, then pointed the correct method at **three of six refs** and concluded *"Board.tsx is on NO ref"* — confidently wrong again, immediately after, opposite cause, same shape. It IS on `lane/control`, 142,430 bytes. **Wrong method, then right method with an incomplete sample.** Either alone reads as bad luck; the pair reads as a discipline gap, and S1 named it against themselves before anyone else could. **And the general rule this is the third instance of today: a check that can only answer YES is not a check** — F-148's self-check could not fail, `check:unreachable` could not see `React.lazy`, and this one cannot report absent. **All three were green, all three were silent, and all three were believed.**| 
| **F-167**| **R-21's dev-server guard has been checking a port nothing ever binds — `lsof -ti:5173` against a server that listens on 8080**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 by S3, verified by S0 before merging.** Every prompt block and every session brief said `lsof -ti:5173` before starting a dev server. **`bun run dev` binds 8080**, via `@lovable.dev/vite-tanstack-config` — S0 confirmed `port: 8080` in the installed package rather than taking the report. So the guard has been asking about **a port that is always free**: a lane checks 5173, sees it clear, starts a second server on 8080, and the two collide. **13 lines across the briefs carried the wrong number.**| **It is not hypothetical and the incident is already on the record.** S2 wrote it up the same day without knowing the cause: their `bun run dev` *"silently fell through to 8081, so two servers were up, which R-21 forbids and this machine has crashed over."* **R-21 exists because this laptop has been driven to a restart by exactly that**, while the founder was asleep — so the guard that was written to prevent it has never once been able to fire. **Third instance today of the same shape (F-166's row names the others): a check that can only answer one way.** F-148's Build self-check could not fail, the zsh idiom could not report absent, and this could not report busy.| **AND THE SECOND HALF IS WORSE THAN THE PORT NUMBER (S1, same day, hit live).** `bun run dev` **does not fail when 8080 is taken.** It prints *"Port 8080 is in use, trying another one..."* and **silently binds 8081** — and on that occasion 8080 was held by `vite dev` from **a different Conductor workspace entirely** (`supaprod-v5/sarajevo`, PID 36515). So `curl localhost:8080` returning 200 proves **a** server is up, not that yours is, and **a browser check on 8080 can drive ANOTHER WORKSPACE'S CODEBASE and report success.** That is worse than a wrong port, because a wrong port fails loudly and this **succeeds quietly against the wrong code** — a lane could "verify" a fix on somebody else's tree and write it up as driven. **It is F-161's contested-worktree hazard reaching the one place a lane calls proof.** **The safe form costs one line: read the dev log's own `Local:` line and drive THAT url** (`bun run dev > dev.log 2>&1 &` then `grep -m1 'Local:' dev.log`). **`lsof` first is necessary and NOT sufficient** — it tells you the port is busy and does nothing to stop vite moving. S1 checked their own earlier drives rather than assuming: `devA.log` records `Local: http://localhost:8080/`, so their server did hold 8080, and every drive rendered strings only their tree contains. **Fixed in all five prompt blocks and all five briefs**, with a note explaining why the number changed so nobody 'corrects' it back. **The wider correction is S3's and it is the durable one:** SESSION-0 tells S0 to keep *two* specified items in every lane queue forever, and **two is the number that produced the stoppage §0.9 fixes** — under §0.9 a lane no longer needs a queue item to work, so the queue is a convenience rather than the work supply. **S0's own queue discipline was part of the defect**, and that is worth keeping in the row rather than in a message.| 
| **F-168**| **THE ONE STATION WHOSE OUTPUT IS A DECISION WAS BRIEFED TO RED-TEAM IT WITH A TOOL THAT REFUSES A DECISION — and it killed the first clean acceptance candidate this product has ever had**| **FIXED 2026-08-31.** Decide's `critic` seat was briefed *"Call `critic.evaluate` on the decision."* `critic.evaluate` takes `target_kind: z.enum(["opportunity", "prd"])` (`registry.server.ts:7254`) and its implementation selects from the `opportunities` or `prds` table and builds a kind-specific prompt (`critic.server.ts:196`). **It cannot accept a decision.** **The sentence survived because its second half is correct** — `decision.revise` exists and does exactly what it says — and **a brief that is half right reads as reasonable**, which is why it shipped 07-07 and was never caught.| **Found by S4 (S4-172) on track `d2263583`, the track S0 created hours earlier to test the acceptance, and the numbers are the argument.** Seven drives, **every one `driven_via='sweep'`, ZERO presses** — the exact shape R-18 asks for — dead at station two holding `nothing-to-hand-on`, with **611,844 tokens spent and 261,771 at Decide alone**. **And it had PRODUCED: six decisions, every one carrying a forecast**, the artifact the whole moat rests on, while the track recorded that it handed on nothing. The critic diagnosed itself in its own output — *"Critic evaluation cannot be performed because critic.evaluate only accepts opportunities or PRDs, not decisions"* — then red-teamed in prose and was marked `completed_with_failures` **for doing the right thing with the only means available**. **Worse than the refusal: the three attempts CONTRADICT each other** (13:40 the evidence confirms the causal claim · 13:50 do-not-build · 14:00 *"we reject the causal claim"*), because the retries fired from the CHECKING seat failing rather than the decision being wrong — so three answers sit on the record with nothing marking which stands, and a forecast graded at Learn would be whichever attempt happened to write last.| **THE BRIEF CHANGED, NOT THE TOOL, and the choice was S0's to make (S4 deliberately did not choose).** Widening `critic.evaluate` looks like a one-line enum edit and is not — it needs a `decisions` branch, a decision-shaped prompt and a persistence path — and **the tool is right as scoped: it red-teams an opportunity or a spec BEFORE a decision exists.** At Decide the artifact IS the decision, and the only two tools that act on one are `decision.record` and `decision.revise`. The new clause names `decision.revise`, and **says why not to call `critic.evaluate`**, because a brief that only forbids invites the seat to relitigate it on every attempt (F-153's lesson: name the thing AND say what it costs). **A guard keeps the collision visible** — it asserts `critic.evaluate` still does NOT accept a decision, so the day somebody builds that feature the test fails and points them at the brief written around its absence, which is F-152's exemption pattern. `CREW_ROLE` is exported so the guard reads the SHAPE rather than scraping the module as text — the instrument that broke twice today the moment a line re-wrapped. **Still open and not mine to close here:** `completed_with_failures` with `failure_kind` NULL on all seven runs, and a hold with `last_hold_because` NULL — it took a database read to diagnose what should have been a glance.| 
| **F-169**| **A FORECAST HELD NO NUMBER. Eleven `forecast_*` columns and not one of them was numeric — which is why the grader has processed zero workspaces in its life**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 (gap #15). The gap was filed as "a forecast is a point and should be a band", and the finding underneath it is worse than that.** Measured on `information_schema`: `decisions` carried `forecast_claim` (text), `forecast_how_we_will_know` (text), `forecast_horizon_date` (a date), and eight more text/date/jsonb columns. **Not one held a number.** So a forecast was PROSE plus a deadline, and the grader could only ask a model to judge a sentence. **F-51's "the grader has processed zero workspaces in its life" is usually read as the grader being unbuilt or unscheduled. It was neither. It had nothing to compute with.**| **Nine columns added in one verified migration** — `forecast_metric`, `forecast_direction`, `forecast_baseline`, `forecast_predicted`, `forecast_band_drifting_at`, `forecast_band_missed_at`, `forecast_observations`, `forecast_if_drifting`, `forecast_if_missed` — all NULLABLE, and **nothing backfills**: 369 decisions predate this and prose plus a date is still a real forecast, so inventing numbers for them would be seeding the one table whose credibility IS the product. **`forecast_direction` is asked for rather than inferred**, because predicted-versus-baseline gets it wrong exactly when the two are equal, which is a real forecast (*hold the line*). **The band lives on the DECISION and there is no `bands.yaml`** — Anthropic's is service-level, ours is per-forecast (`SPEC-STATION-MODEL` §2.6).| **The two things the logic has to get right, both tested (14 tests).** (1) **`unknown` whenever it cannot answer** — no reading, no direction, a missing threshold, or **thresholds ordered against their own direction**, which is malformed rather than merely odd; a band that guesses is worse than no band because a tier fires off it. (2) **A THIN BAND NEVER OPENS WORK.** `forecast_observations` is the honesty guard as a VALUE rather than a convention, and below three readings a miss is logged rather than actioned — §3 B: *a tier that fires on noise is theatre and gets the feature deleted rather than fixed.* Also kept: **drifting diagnoses READ-ONLY and does not open work**, because a drift that opens a change every time trains a person to ignore it. **Shipped OPTIONAL on `decision.record`, deliberately**, following the precedent two screens above it in that same file: the three forecast fields shipped optional on 2026-08-20 and became required on 08-22 once the seat was filling them. **Requiring a number on day one is how an agent learns to fabricate one**, and a fabricated baseline is worse than prose because something acts on it. The strategist brief asks for the band and says plainly to leave it out rather than invent it. **Gap #15's tier-3 action is gap #4's return edge**, built the same day. **AND THE DEPENDENCY IS STATED HERE SO NOBODY BUILDS THE NEXT PIECE OUT OF ORDER.** The band can be WRITTEN today and cannot yet be EVALUATED, because evaluating it needs a *reading* at the horizon and **nothing produces one**: that is Decide's metric probe (gap #11, `SPEC-BUILD-PATHS.md` §2.3), which is unbuilt and which that spec already ranks **first of all the sandbox uses** precisely because *"without it the verdict can never land"*. **Wiring band evaluation into the settlement matrix before the probe exists would be building a reader for data that cannot arrive** — which is F-147's shape (a finished thing briefed at zero stations), F-155's shape (work that provably cannot move) and F-160's shape (a consumer with no caller), all of which were found and filed the same day. **S0 stopped short of it deliberately rather than leaving a half-wired reader behind.** **The order is therefore: Decide's metric probe, THEN the band reader in `classifyOutcomeSettlement`.** That matrix already reasons carefully about *"when a decisive verdict has no number behind it"*, so the band is an input it is shaped to take — but only once a number exists to give it.| 
| **F-170**| **The first document a lane reads promised NINE reference images at a path that does not exist, and there were ZERO anywhere**| **FIXED 2026-08-31.** `START-HERE.md:67` — the first file every session is told to read — carried a row pointing at `../design-reference/mobbin-2026-08/` and claiming ***"Nine reference images, committed — lanes have no Mobbin access."*** **That path has never existed.** And the directory that DOES exist, `docs/design/reference-2026-08-26/`, held **one `.gitkeep` and nothing else.** So the claim was false twice over: wrong path, and zero images at either. SESSION-0 flagged the path half as far back as its own §1 — *"which does not exist. Fix that claim or create the directory; a lane cannot design against a path that is not there"* — **and S0 had not done it.** *"A lane blocked on a reference is S0's failure"* is in the same brief.| **The row is corrected in place and now points at the real directory, which is no longer empty.** S0 pulled the board surface from Mobbin — the right one to pull first, because A07 has just made the board the **only** surface a signed-in person can land on, and §0.7 measures the sixty seconds **signed in**. Committed as **mechanics in words with every source linked**, per R-20 §7 (*port mechanics, never screenshots — a screenshot loses the mechanics, which is the only part worth having*). Five to take, three to refuse, each with its reason. **`docs:check` refused the commit until the new file was linked from its folder index**, which is the anti-rot gate doing exactly its job.| **And the pull recorded what it did NOT find, which is the part worth keeping.** Across six sources — Qatalog, Slack Lists, Wrike, GitHub Projects, Airtable, ClickUp — **not one shows a row being worked on by something that is not a person.** Every board in the reference set is work assigned to humans and waiting. **So the live-agent case is genuinely unreferenced on Mobbin**, and the next session to go looking should not spend the pull discovering that again; the closest prior art is `docs/research/agentic-product-patterns-2026-08.md` §1. **A negative result from a reference pull is worth committing for the same reason a FALSE row is worth keeping in this ledger.**| 
| **F-171**| **The return edge failed on every insert, reported `ok` in 817ms, and the reason reached nobody — the same defect it was built to help fix**| **FIXED 2026-08-31, and S0 shipped it in its own code within the hour of filing three others of the same shape.** The return edge (gap #4) went live at 14:30 and `outcome-tick` ran at 15:00:00. `cron.job_run_details`: **succeeded**. `job_runs`: **status `ok`, 817ms, no error**. `error_events` for `cron.outcome-tick`: **none, ever**. And `spine_tracks` with a `from_learning_id`: **zero**, against a record that owed exactly one. **Three separate observability surfaces all said fine.**| **THE CAUSE IS A DEPLOYMENT FACT THIS CODEBASE HAD ALREADY NAMED.** `from_learning_id` was minutes old when the tick ran, and **PostgREST caches its schema** — so the column existed in Postgres and did not exist to the client, which fails the insert with **`PGRST204`**. `src/lib/read-failure.ts` already classifies `42703`/`PGRST204` as a **deployment** fact rather than a runtime one; the rule was written down and the new code did not use it. **Proved by elimination rather than guessed:** S0 ran the pass's own reads against the live database (both misses returned), then ran the identical INSERT in raw SQL — **it succeeded**, which rules out constraints, RLS and the row shape and leaves the client's schema cache as the only difference. `NOTIFY pgrst, 'reload schema'` issued.| **THE REAL DEFECT IS NOT THE CACHE, IT IS THAT NOTHING COULD SEE IT.** The insert error went into `result.skipped`, which travels back **in the tick's HTTP response body — and nothing stores that body.** `job_runs` keeps status and duration; the response is discarded. So the edge could fail on every insert, every hour, forever, and the only evidence would be a zero in a JSON nobody reads. **That is the defect S0 filed three times the same day** — F-148's self-check that could not fail, F-166's idiom that could not report absent, F-167's guard that could not report busy — **and then shipped.** The tick's own file header even says it: *"a console.error in a Worker reaches nobody the founder can read."* **Fixed: a skipped insert now writes an `error_events` row.** It stays best-effort, because a failed insert must not cost the grader its settle — **but best-effort has never meant unobservable.** **The standing lesson: after a migration adds a column, the schema cache is a step, not an implementation detail** — and the first write through the client is the thing to check, not the ALTER.| 
| **F-172**| **CORRECTED: Discover's three-seat crew is SLOW, not stuck — it takes ~50 minutes and five sweep drives to clear one station, because a 3-seat crew does not fit one drive's clock**| **CORRECTED WITHIN THE HOUR BY THE TRACK ITSELF, AND THE HEADLINE I FILED WAS WRONG.** This row first said the crew *"re-runs instead of completing"* and that the station *"has never handed on"*. **At 15:30:36 it handed on** — `sense → decide`, `driven_via='sweep'`, `actor='system'` — so Discover completes; it is simply SLOW. I was careful not to claim the mechanism and I was not careful enough about the headline, which is the same error in a smaller place: **"has not yet" is a measurement and "never" is a claim**, and I wrote the second while holding only the first. **What survives is the real finding and it still matters:** clearing ONE station took **five sweep drives across ~50 minutes**. At that rate a seven-station acceptance run is a multi-hour affair, which is why three months of ten-minute ticks have never produced one. **The cause is structural and is F-14's shape without F-14's fatal outcome.** Discover's crew is three seats at 15–40s each against one drive's deadline, so a full pass never fits and the sweep resumes it every ten minutes. F-14's own note calls this out for a TWO-seat crew — *"the split was not bad luck, it was structural for any crew with a slow first seat"* — and the reason it is no longer fatal is `didStationProduce`, which stopped the finishing tick reading `attached.length === 0` as `produced-nothing`. **That fix is doing its job here, visibly.** **OPEN, MEASURED, AND THE MECHANISM OF THE SLOWNESS IS PROVEN WHERE THE EARLIER CLAIM WAS NOT.** After F-168 was fixed and deployed, the acceptance candidate `d2263583` stopped dying at Decide and now cycles at Discover holding `out-of-time`. S1 drove it and confirmed the surface reads truthfully throughout; the driver is S0's. Measured: `agent_runs` by seat on that one track — **`discovery-scout` 6, `researcher` 6, `customer-insights` 5** — against **`station_drives` 5** and `seat_cursor` **2** on a three-seat crew. **A crew that completed would run each seat once per pass; this has run seats 0 and 1 six times each without the station ever finishing.**| **What the seats actually said, which is a second finding inside the first.** `discovery-scout` FINDS and logs evidence (at 15:20 it logged a new signal `9bef6971`; at 15:00 it reported the evidence *"already on record"*). Then `researcher` and `customer-insights` each report **"No user-sourced evidence exists in the workspace"** — for the same track, minutes later. **All three return `status='completed'` with `failure_kind` NULL.** So one seat produces and two contradict it, and nothing on the station reconciles them. Individual runs take 15–40s and one recorded *"Reached the step limit before finishing"*, so a three-seat pass does not fit inside one drive's budget and the sweep resumes it every ten minutes.| **THE HYPOTHESIS I HAD WAS ALSO WRONG, AND CHECKING IT IS WHAT STOPPED IT REACHING THIS ROW.** I reasoned that `resumeSeatFrom(3, 3)` returns 0, so a drive running out of clock after the LAST seat would wrap the cursor and restart the crew. **False:** the deadline check sits INSIDE the seat loop and BEFORE each seat, so `ranLongAtSeat = seatIndex` can never exceed `crew.length - 1`, and `ranLong` is assigned in exactly one place. I also read `updated_at` frozen at 14:31 as evidence the cursor write was not landing — **also false**: that update writes `spend_used_usd` and `seat_cursor` and deliberately does not touch `updated_at`, and spend has moved to $0.2969, so the write lands every drive. **Two plausible mechanisms, both disproved by reading the code rather than the symptom.** F-14 is the nearest prior art (*a slow seat splitting the crew across ticks*, fixed with `didStationProduce`) and **it is explicitly NOT ruled out or ruled in here.** The next session should prove the cursor's write path before changing it: **this is the ~46-track `sense` graveyard, and it is the first time it has been observable on a track with zero presses and zero answered approvals confounding the reading.** Guessing at it now would spend that instrument.| 
| **F-173**| **Decide filed two decisions and the advance was refused for having no decision — the check contradicts the record it is checking**| **OPEN, and it is the acceptance blocker as of 2026-08-31 15:43. F-168's fix WORKED and this is the wall behind it.** Under the corrected brief the Decide crew did exactly its job: `strategist` recorded decision `af6bb80c` (*"Do not attribute tablet checkout abandonment to address layout without A/B isolation"*) and **the `critic` then filed a second decision `6752bdf6` at 15:41:27** — which is `decision.revise` being called instead of the seat dying on a tool that refuses a decision. **Both are `spine_track_members` rows with `artifact_kind='decision'` and `station='decide'`.** `STATION_NEEDS.define` is `kinds: ["decision"]`. **And the driver held the track `nothing-to-hand-on`.** **CORRECTION, 2026-08-31, and it is mine and it is the worst kind:** this row first quoted a hold line — *"decide filed something, but define still has no a recorded decision to write the spec against"* — **as though the record said it. The record says nothing. `last_hold_because` is EMPTY.** I read that sentence off the TEMPLATE in `driver.server.ts:2917` and presented it as evidence, **which is fabricating a quote in the one document whose whole purpose is that claims carry their measurement** — filed within an hour of correcting myself for a softer version of the same thing in F-172. The hold is real; the sentence was not.| **Measured on the live acceptance candidate `d2263583`, which is clean: 17 drives, every one `driven_via='sweep'`, ZERO presses, ZERO non-sweep stage events.** The refusal comes from `driver.server.ts:2900`, `!needIsMet(STATION_NEEDS[arrivedAt], filedNow)`, where `filedNow = [...filed, ...attached.map(a => a.artifactKind)]` and `needIsMet` is a plain `kinds.some(k => filed.includes(k))`. **AND THE BRANCH I BLAMED APPEARS NOT TO FIT, measured after the correction above.** `filed` comes from `loadUpstream`, which reads `spine_track_members` **track-wide** filtered on `superseded_at IS NULL`. Measured on this track: **10 decision members, 10 live, 0 superseded.** So `filed` contains `"decision"` ten times over and `needIsMet(STATION_NEEDS.define, filedNow)` should be TRUE — **which means the `:2900` branch is probably NOT the one that fired, and my whole diagnosis pointed at the wrong line.** The only other writer of this hold is the Build branch at `:2882`, gated on `station === "build"`, and this track is at `decide`. **ANSWERED BY F-174, filed immediately below: the hold's origin is the DECLINE PATH.** The newest decision is `declined`, so the driver waives `define/design/build/ship`, `arrivedAt` becomes **`learn`**, and `STATION_NEEDS.learn` wants `prd`/`changeset`/`deployment` — none of which a track that was correctly never built can hold. So the `:2900` branch DID fire; I was wrong that it did not, and wrong about which station it was refusing entry to. **The record was never contradicted — I was reading the wrong destination.** *(Superseded reading kept below for the arc: at the time this read as)* **the hold's origin being genuinely unaccounted for**, and that is the finding: not "the check contradicts the record" but **"a hold was written that no branch I can find explains, and it left no reason behind it."** Also on the record and not yet explained: both seats returned **`completed_with_failures` with `failure_kind` NULL**, and `last_hold_because` is EMPTY — so the hold cannot say why, which is the same NULL-reason hole S4 filed against `tools-refused`, `given-up` and `going-in-circles`.| **NOT FIXED. AND THE REAL LESSON OF THIS ROW IS THE THREE CORRECTIONS INSIDE IT, ALL MINE, ALL ON ONE TRACK IN NINETY MINUTES:** F-172's *"never hands on"* about a station that then handed on · the fabricated hold line above · and a diagnosis aimed at a branch the data says did not fire. **Every one came from reasoning off the source instead of reading the row, on the one track where the row is finally readable.** The empty `last_hold_because` is what made all three possible, which promotes it from a nuisance to the actual defect: **a hold that cannot say why invites the reader to invent a reason, and I did, three times.** Candidate explanations for the hold, none proven: (1) `attached` did not carry the two decisions at the moment of the check, which would make this **F-14's crew-split shape at the ADVANCE gate rather than at the self-check** — the seats ran 36s and 43s against one drive's deadline, so the tick plainly ran long; (2) `filed` is arrival-scoped and was captured before the crew ran, which is correct by design, leaving `attached` as the only carrier. **The next session should prove which by instrumenting `filedNow` at that call site rather than reasoning from the symptom, and should NOT change `needIsMet`, which is three lines and behaving exactly as written.** This is the first time the loop has been watchable this far in with nothing confounding the reading — spending that instrument on a guess is the one thing not to do.| 
| **F-174**| **A "do not build" decision waives the four middle stations, lands on Learn, and Learn requires an artifact a track that was correctly never built can never have. THE DECLINE PATH CANNOT COMPLETE.**| **FIXED 2026-08-31, deployed and verified live (`2e2f59f0…` → `026b7621…`, 16:14:30Z); the resolution is in the last column. Status corrected in place 2026-08-31 by S0 after S1 reported this cell still read OPEN while the resolution cell already said FIXED — verified three ways before reporting it (the source arm, the guard in `handing-on.test.ts:208`, and `git merge-base --is-ancestor dd3e47a84 origin/main`). It is the complete mechanism behind F-173 — the hold whose origin I could not account for.** Traced end to end in the source and confirmed against the rows. At Decide, `decisionWasRefusal` (`driver.server.ts:3555`) reads the **newest** decision member and returns true when its `status = 'declined'` — which is what `decision.record` writes for `call: "do-not-build"`. The driver then **waives `define`, `design`, `build` and `ship`** with the reason *"The call was not to build, so there is nothing to specify or ship"*, which is correct and humane. **`nextStation` therefore returns `learn`.** And `STATION_NEEDS.learn` is `kinds: ["prd", "changeset", "deployment"]`. **A track whose call was NOT TO BUILD has no spec, no changeset and no deployment — by construction, because not building them is the decision.** So `needIsMet` fails, the advance is refused as `nothing-to-hand-on`, an attempt is counted, and the track is stuck in the one place the product should be proudest of.| **Measured 2026-08-31 on the live acceptance candidate `d2263583` and then across the whole table.** The track holds **10 live decision members: the FIRST approved (13:40:29) and every one after it declined** — the crew re-deciding the same question because the route will not let it leave. S1 drove the surface and found the declines land in PAIRS about a minute apart, twice per sweep, and corrected their own copy from *"Decide filed 8 decisions"* to *"filed 10 decisions, 7 of them repeat 2 things already filed"* — refusing to dedupe it away, because **how often a station re-files the same thing is the fact that reveals the jam.** Population check: **exactly ONE track in the database has a declined newest decision.** So this is not a widespread stall — **it is the first time a "do not build" has ever reached this point in the product, and the path dead-ends the first time it is walked.**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 under the founder's standing authority (*"don't keep it open… you make the calls"*), and it is resolution (2).** When `routeDeclined` and the next station is `learn`, the advance check now asks for **the decision whose forecast Learn would grade** instead of a prd/changeset/deployment. **Scoped to `routeDeclined` so it cannot loosen the ordinary path** — a track that WAS built still has to arrive at Learn with something built — and the hold line now names the need actually used rather than the station's default. Two guards added: the decline arm exists and is conditioned on `routeDeclined`, and the ordinary path still reads `STATION_NEEDS[arrivedAt]`, so the arm cannot quietly become the only branch. **`tsc` 0, 13,296 pass / 0 fail.** **Two source-text assertions in `handing-on.test.ts` broke on the refactor and were repointed to the PREDICATE rather than the argument spelling** — the third false red from a source scan in one day (F-166, F-152), and the fix is the same one each time: assert what the rule is, not how the call is written. **(1) and (3) were both refused, and (3) explicitly:** closing the route silently loses the record, and **a forecast attached to a NO is still a forecast** — grading it is the whole moat, and a verdict of *"we said no, and here is what happened instead"* is one this product should want more than most. **The original framing is kept below because the reasoning is the artifact:** `decision.record`'s own comment records the previous half of this: a *"no"* used to be stored `status: "approved"`, so *"the spine read an approved decision and walked the track on to Define, Design and Build, to construct the thing the decision had just refused — the one station whose job is to stop work could not."* **That was fixed by making the refusal expressible. This is the same sentence one layer on: the refusal is now expressible and still cannot COMPLETE.** Three candidate resolutions, and they differ in what a decline MEANS: (1) a declined track is **done** at Decide and should close, not walk to Learn — cheapest, and arguably right, since there is no outcome to grade; (2) Learn's precondition gains a decline arm, so a refusal can be graded as *"we said no, and here is what happened instead"* — the most valuable, because a forecast attached to a **no** is still a forecast and grading it is the moat; (3) the decline waives Learn too, which closes the route silently and **loses the record**, so it is named here in order to be refused. **Do not pick (3).**| 
| **F-175**| **A hold that cannot say why invites the reader to invent a reason — six hold sites computed a specific sentence and persisted only the coarse word**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 by S0, and it is the defect that made F-173's fabrication available.** `driver.server.ts` writes `last_hold` at ten sites. Six of them build a sentence that exists nowhere else — `self-check-failed` carries the verifier's reason, `needs-evidence` the forecast's horizon date, `produced-nothing` the last failing tool and its words (the whole of what F-87 went and fetched), `nothing-to-hand-on` at one site which two calls would clear it and at the other what the next station is short of, and `stalled` the thrown error — **and every one returned that sentence to the caller and let it die with the tick.** Each now builds it into one local written to BOTH the row and the screen, so the two cannot drift.| **Measured before the fix: 97 held tracks, EXACTLY ONE carrying a `last_hold_because`.** Grouped: `station-cannot-finish` 36 rows / 0 reasons · `out-of-time` 28 / 0 · `produced-nothing` 12 / 0 · `needs-evidence` 12 / 0 · `going-in-circles` 3 / 0 · `given-up` 2 / 0 · `nothing-to-hand-on` 1 / 0 · `waiting-on-a-person` 1 / 0 · `tools-refused` 1 / 0 · `needs-a-waived-station` 1 / **1**. **A SECOND DEFECT WAS HYPOTHESISED AND THE DATA REFUSED IT, so it is not filed:** those sites left the column untouched, so a new hold word could inherit the PREVIOUS hold's reason — the rows say it has never happened, because almost nothing ever wrote the column at all. The fix writes it unconditionally, which forecloses the staleness without claiming it occurred.| **F-127's exemption is KEPT and argued rather than waived.** A site whose line is `HOLD_LINE[hold]` verbatim must still write NOTHING: a generic sentence in a column meant for specifics reads as a reason while being derivable from the word beside it, and **null is readable as "no more was said" where a generic line is not.** Three literal holds qualify (`out-of-time`, `over-budget`, `waiting-on-a-person`) and so does `decideCorrection`'s branch, whose hold is dynamic; the guard names all four as expressions and requires each to STAY silent, so this cannot be loosened by accident in either direction. **The guard is STRUCTURAL, not textual** — it splits the driver on `.update(` and asks whether each object setting `last_hold` also sets `last_hold_because`, so rewording a line leaves it green and deleting the write turns it red. **That choice was earned the same day: a pre-existing source-text assertion broke on the hoist, the FOURTH in one day to die on a behaviour-preserving edit** (F-166, F-152, `handing-on.test.ts` ×2). It was repointed to the whole branch, whitespace-normalised, with `expect(to).toBeGreaterThan(from)` because a missed anchor returns `-1` and `slice(from, -1)` is nearly the whole file — which passes every assertion vacuously.|
| **F-176**| **R-21's dev-server guard counts BROWSER TABS as a running server — the third distinct way this one check has been wrong**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 by S0 across five briefs and the master prompt. Found by S2.** Every lane runs `lsof -ti:8080` before starting a dev server. **That form matches every socket with 8080 at either end, so an open browser tab pointed at a dev server counts as "busy"** — including the lane's own tab, and including a tab left over from a server that is already dead. So a lane reads "busy", declines to start a server, and is blocked by a browser. **The comment on that very line has always read *"If anything is LISTENING, do not start another"* — the intent was right and the command never implemented it.** One flag fixes it: `-sTCP:LISTEN`.| **S2 measured it live while killing their own server: one `vite dev` and TWO `Google Chrome Helper` pids, one of them in a different Conductor workspace.** S0 proved the mechanism under controlled conditions rather than accepting the report — a listener and a separate client process on one port: **plain form returns both pids, `-sTCP:LISTEN` returns only the listener.** Applied to the `kill` line too, where it matters more: the plain form would hand `kill` the pid of a browser, or of another workspace's server the lane never started.| **AND IT RETIRES A CLAIM S2 MADE EARLIER THE SAME DAY, IN THE DIRECTION THAT LETS THEM OFF, WHICH IS WHY THEY RAISED IT.** S2 had reported that another workspace's server held 8080 all session and that their `pkill -f "vite dev"` may have killed it. What they read as a server was very possibly a Chrome helper with a cwd in another workspace. **They did NOT convert that into "the pkill was harmless"** — the pattern kill was still wrong and which way it went is still unproven; what changed is that the evidence for "another lane had a server up" was never evidence of that. **Third instance of one shape for this single guard: F-167 had the wrong port number (5173, always free, so it could never report busy), then vite's silent fall-through to 8081 meant a lane could verify a fix against another workspace's tree, and now the check reports busy when nothing is listening.** A guard that has been wrong three ways in one file is a guard nobody has ever watched fail.|
| **F-178**| **A waived station leaves no mark on the row, so `waived = '[]'` stopped meaning "it walked all seven" — on the acceptance query, the hour the first clean candidate reached Learn**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 by S0, minutes after finding it, and I opened the hole myself in F-174 the same afternoon.** The decline path builds its waivers with `waive()` into an in-memory `onwardRoute` and **the row was never told.** It was recomputed from scratch every tick, so the loop behaved correctly and the record said nothing had happened. The driver now persists `onwardRoute.waived` whenever a tick changes it — which fixes both directions, the decline that ADDS waivers and the reopen that REMOVES them.| **Measured on `d2263583`, the live acceptance candidate, at 17:31:31 the moment it arrived.** It walked `sense -> decide -> learn` with every drive `driven_via='sweep'`, **zero presses, zero approvals raised, on a real workspace** — and it was **DRIVEN AT TWO STATIONS OF SEVEN**, `define`/`design`/`build`/`ship` having been waived by the decline. Its `waived` column read `[]`. **R-18's query carries that clause for exactly one purpose — to guarantee the work went through all seven — and a track that skipped four satisfied it.** `path` still read all seven, which is the intended route rather than the walked one, so nothing on the row contradicted the claim. **F-174 IS WHAT MADE THE ROW REACHABLE:** before it, a declined track stuck at Decide and could never arrive at Learn to be miscounted. *A fix that unblocks a path can invalidate a measurement that only held while the path was blocked* — and this is the measurement the whole project is judged by.| **AND THE SECOND CONSEQUENCE IS WORSE THAN THE FIRST.** The waiver block promises in its own comment that if the verdict later shows the refusal was wrong, *"the stations come back rather than needing a person to remember this happened"*. `reopenIfOutcomeContested` opens `if (!route.waived.some(w => w.reopensWhen === "outcome-contested")) return route;` and `route.waived` is read from `row.waived` — **which was always `[]`, so the trigger could never match and the promise could never be kept.** The feature was written, tested, commented and dead. **Rows written before this fix still carry the wrong `[]`**, so the operating model now tells a reader to cross-check any non-zero acceptance against `track_drives`: seven stations driven, or it did not walk seven. Guard is structural — the update objects that move a track and that finish a route must both be able to carry `waived` — so rewording a comment leaves it green and dropping the write turns it red.|
| **F-179**| **A CI fix run that commits nothing spends no budget and retires its head sha for ever — so Tier 0.2's unblock worked, produced two dead changesets, and every instrument reported healthy**| **FIXED 2026-08-31 by S0, found by checking whether §M move 3 had actually done anything.** `fix_attempts` was reset to 0 on the changesets behind PRs #2 and #3 at 09:29 and 09:32 UTC, and `ci-poll-tick` dispatched a builder run for each **within seconds — the unblock worked.** Both runs finished `completed` having called `studio.fix.commit`, which the **pre-F-152** policy floor turned into an approval rather than a commit. **Both approvals were still `pending` and undecided eight hours later**, and `MODE_RULED_ABOVE_WINS` did not land until 10:36 UTC — an hour after they were raised — so they are artifacts of code that no longer exists.| **TWO COUNTERS DISAGREED AND THE WRONG ONE BLOCKED.** The tick's own comment states the design: *"budget consumption lives in `studio.fix.commit` itself (per real commit); the dispatch is bounded by the head-sha dedup above."* So a run that ends without committing **spends no budget** — `fix_attempts` stayed **0**, so the exhausted-park never fired and nothing reported it — **and retires its head sha for ever**, because the sha only moves when a commit lands, and BOTH the `agent_runs` `like` count (`> 0`) and the `ci-poll.fix-dispatch` claim treated *was ever dispatched* as *is finished*. **The changeset is dead and every instrument reads healthy:** `status='pr_open'`, `fix_attempts: 0`, and `job_runs` recording `ok` for `cron.ci-poll-tick` every two minutes at ~4.2s of real work. Measured: 240 ticks in eight hours, zero movement.| **The bound stays; its ceiling moves from 1 to `CI_FIX_BUDGET`**, so F-151's spin remains impossible — three dispatches per red head, then it stops — and the claim key gains the ordinal, **because keyed by sha alone it was a SECOND permanent retirement and raising the ceiling alone would have changed nothing.** When it does stop with nothing committed, it **records an error event**: the tick's `failures` array is returned to its caller and `job_runs` has no payload column, so every counter this tick reports otherwise reaches nobody — **the F-171 shape one layer up, on the Build→Ship seam the acceptance runs through.** The two stale approvals are deliberately NOT hand-answered: they expire 2026-09-03, and answering a boundary call to prove a loop works proves the opposite. **This is the third finding in one day whose shape is a terminal state no counter can express** (F-175's silent hold, F-178's unrecorded waiver, this). **F-178 CONFIRMED INDEPENDENTLY BY S4 (S4-175) AND THE CLAUSE THEY HANDED BACK IS ADOPTED.** The driver fix repairs rows written after it and can do nothing for rows already on disk, so the honest query now ASKS the walk instead of inferring it: `count(DISTINCT station) FROM track_drives = 7`. **Verified before adopting: it passes `d1168015` (7 driven) and fails `d2263583` (3 — sense, decide, learn).** S4's count of three is right and my "two of seven" was right when measured — Learn has been driven twice since. **It is safe only because `waived = '[]'` is already required**: a track that legitimately waives a station must not be asked for seven, and this only ever runs against tracks that waived nothing. **S4's sharper point is the one to keep: F-131's `status='done'` was added for an unrelated reason and is now the ONLY guard standing between that track and a false acceptance** — the accidental-guard shape, arriving on the clause that matters most.|
| **F-180**| **`499 request_cancelled` ON THIS DATABASE PATH DOES NOT MEAN THE STATEMENT FAILED — it can land after you have verified it did not, and I filed two wrong diagnoses before measuring that**| **OPEN, and it is FOUNDER-FACING. Reproduced four times on 2026-09-01, three different statements, schema verified unchanged after each.** `CREATE TABLE public.track_hold_notices (…)` succeeded and every column and both indexes verified from `information_schema` and `pg_indexes`. Then **`ALTER TABLE … ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` returned `499 request_cancelled` three times** — twice bare, once wrapped in a `DO $$ … EXECUTE $$` block — with `pg_class.relrowsecurity` still `false` after each. **`ALTER TABLE user_notification_preferences ADD COLUMN email_stopped …` was cancelled the same way**, on a different table, with the column absent afterwards. `DROP TABLE` then succeeded first time.| **Lock contention is ruled out on the decisive case.** `track_hold_notices` was seconds old, held zero rows, had no reader, no writer and no other session touching it, so nothing could have been holding the `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` that `ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` needs. The same statement class failed on an in-use table too, and the two statements that are NOT `ALTER` both succeeded on the first attempt. **The discriminator is the statement, not the table and not the lock.**| **THE TABLE WAS DROPPED RATHER THAN LEFT STANDING, and that is the decision worth recording.** `public` is exposed through PostgREST, so an un-RLS'd table there is readable by any authenticated user the moment it holds a row — and this one carries `user_id` and `track_id` for every piece of stopped work. It held zero rows and had no writer, so nothing leaked; leaving it would have meant a multi-tenant table in production that I could create and could not secure, waiting for the first writer to make it a real exposure. **A table I cannot put RLS on is a table that must not exist.** Both migrations are written, reviewed and committed to `supabase/migrations/` — the notices table with its policy, and the `email_stopped` preference — so the apply is one unblocked session away. **S3 was told the table does NOT exist and must not be built against**, which is what `S0-A02` §3 promised: *"until you see those rows from me, treat the table as not existing."* **This blocks gap #2's delivery record, which is S3's job 1.** **RE-TESTED AT CLOSE-OUT, 2026-09-01, AND THE FIRST DIAGNOSIS WAS TOO BROAD.** Applying the two migrations one at a time as the founder's close-out asks: **`ALTER TABLE user_notification_preferences ADD COLUMN email_stopped` SUCCEEDED**, verified from `information_schema` (`boolean`, `NOT NULL`, default `true`). **`CREATE TABLE track_hold_notices` succeeded** (6 columns, both indexes). **`ALTER TABLE … ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` SUCCEEDED** — `pg_class.relrowsecurity` is now `true` — **the same statement that was cancelled three times four hours earlier.** So `ALTER` was never blocked as a class; something transient was. **`CREATE POLICY` is what is actually refused: three attempts, bare and wrapped in a `DO $$ … EXECUTE $$` guard, all `499 request_cancelled`, `pg_policies` still 0.** **AND THIS TIME THE TABLE STAYS, because the safety argument inverted:** RLS enabled with zero policies is **deny-all** to every authenticated client, so there is no exposure — the opposite of the un-RLS'd table I dropped at 01:00, which was readable by anyone the moment it held a row. The cost is now functional rather than a leak: an owner cannot read their own notices, on a feature that has no reader yet. **What the founder still owns: why `CREATE POLICY` is refused, and whether the earlier `ALTER` cancellations were the same cause or a passing one.** Naming a whole statement class from four failures in one window was the error, and the correction is mine. **CLOSED 2026-09-01 AT 04:00, AND BOTH OF MY EARLIER DIAGNOSES WERE WRONG.** Verified on `information_schema`, `pg_class` and `pg_policies` at close-out: **the migration is FULLY APPLIED** — `track_hold_notices` exists with 6 columns, `relrowsecurity = true`, and **the policy `track_hold_notices_select_own` (`SELECT`, `auth.uid() = user_id`) IS PRESENT** — plus `user_notification_preferences.email_stopped`. **Every statement I reported as refused has landed.** First I named a whole statement class (*"`ALTER` is refused"*) from four failures in one window; then I narrowed it to *"only `CREATE POLICY` is refused"* from three more. **Both were false and the second was false at the moment I wrote it.** **THE REAL FINDING IS OPERATIONAL AND IT IS WORTH MORE THAN EITHER WRONG ONE: a `499 request_cancelled` from this path is a CLIENT-SIDE timeout, not a server-side refusal, and the statement can complete afterwards.** Every one of my verifications was honest — `relrowsecurity` really was `false`, `pg_policies` really was `0` when I read them — **so verify-after-each is necessary and NOT SUFFICIENT: it proves what was true at that instant, not what the statement will have done.** The migration rule in `SESSION-0-CONDUCTOR.md` should say so. **And the cost of getting this wrong was real: at 01:00 I DROPPED a correctly-created table because RLS appeared not to have applied**, which may simply have been early. Re-creating it cost nothing here; on a table with rows it would have cost the rows.|

It is not necessarily a bug — an answered approval whose run carries no `track_id` may genuinely not belong to a track, **and nobody has shown that any of the other 175 does.** And the acceptance is not unguarded, because the press clause is independent and works. **What is certainly true is that nobody knew the reach was 1**, while `CLAUDE.md` presents it as *"F-79 as a join rather than a sentence someone must recall"* — a phrasing that reads as though it screens the whole population.| **IT MATTERS AT EXACTLY THE WRONG MOMENT.** The first time a track walks all seven stations, this clause is what has to prove no person answered a boundary call on it — and an answered call that never got a `mission_id` leaves the query reporting a clean unattended run. **Not changed tonight**, deliberately: the fix depends on whether those 175 are genuinely track-less, which is a judgement about the data rather than a defect in the SQL, and rewriting the acceptance query at 03:00 on the night a candidate is live is the worst possible time to touch it. **HOW IT WAS FOUND IS THE MOST USEFUL PART:** S3 and S4 disagreed about a number that did not matter (undecided approvals on one workspace, 77 against 9) and **neither reproduced** — S3's join counted approval-by-run PAIRS where one mission holds 28 runs, so 7 became 77, and S3 retracted it in all three of their documents. **Chasing a discrepancy neither could reproduce is what put the join under a microscope.** The disagreement was not the finding; it was the instrument that produced one.|| **F-188**| **A guard that reads a source file matches the COMMENT that explains the fix, so a correct fix fails it and the only way to pass is to delete the explanation**| **FIXED 2026-09-03 by A2, in the two packets that hit it (P-54, P-56).** This repo writes its reasoning into the file beside the code, and it writes guards that assert against the file's text. Those two habits collide, and the collision is silent until the day someone fixes a defect properly: the comment saying *"this used to read `${n} decisions are ready for you`"* is matched by a guard asserting that string is gone. I wrote it twice in one day. **The failure mode is what makes it worth a number: the cheapest way to make the guard pass is to remove the paragraph explaining why the code changed**, so the guard actively destroys the record it was written to protect. **The fix is to strip comments before asserting, AND to assert the stripped source still contains something real** — otherwise a stripper that eats the file makes every "not.toContain" pass vacuously, which is a green suite proving nothing. Three sibling mistakes from the same two packets, all mine, all caught by the guard failing rather than by review: a slice terminated at `indexOf("})")` that stopped early on the `}` inside `(refused[0] as { workspace_id?: string \| null })` and reported a correct call site as missing its argument (brace-match instead); `not.toContain("0 ")` against a string containing *"10 assumption challenges"*; and a guard banning `waitingOnPerson.length ===` when the defect is RENDERING the count and `length === 0` only picks a branch. **The rule: assert against code with comments stripped, prove the strip left something, and ban what the defect DOES rather than the identifier it happens to use.** **A sibling from the same family, A3, P-53's first push (2026-09-04):** a `tsc --noEmit` backgrounded with `&` and read from its log file immediately after, which returned empty and was reported as clean — the job was still running, not finished, so the read proved nothing rather than proving green. Same shape as the rest of this row: a check whose "pass" and "has not run yet" look identical is not a check. Pushed a real type error (A2's P-50 branded `AskQuestion`) that A1 caught on the merged tip. Fixed going forward: run `tsc` synchronously (or wait on the background job's own completion notification) and read the log's line count / exit code before calling anything clean. |
| **F-189**| **A guard that asserts a COUNT where it means a RULE: it breaks when nothing is wrong, and the cheapest way to pass it is to retype the number**| **FIXED 2026-09-04 by A2 across twelve guards in six files (P-60).** Adding six rail doors broke `expect(PRIMARY_NAV.length).toBe(3)`, `expect(JUMP_DESTINATIONS.length).toBe(3)`, `expect(navChords().length).toBe(3)`, `expect(labels).toHaveLength(2)`, `expect(owns.length).toBe(2)`, `expect(BOUND.length).toBe(3)`, `expect(rows).toEqual(["/track"])`, `expect(keyed).toEqual(["/start","/track"])` and four more — **not one of which described a defect**. Every one sat directly above or below the assertion that WAS the rule (`new Set(hints).size === hints.length`, `railOwnerOf(r) === r`, `unkeyed === []`), and every one of those passed untouched. **The failure mode is what earns the row: a red suite that is telling you nothing, whose cheapest repair is to edit the number until it matches, which trains the next person to treat a failing guard as arithmetic.** Worse, one was a literal second copy of the thing under test (`const RAIL_DOORS = ["/start", "/track"]` in `palette-catalog.test.ts`) — the drift the nav model was built to make impossible, living in the test that checks for drift. **The rule: assert the INVARIANT and derive any count from the list you are checking** (`toBe(PRIMARY_NAV.length)`, `toBe(RAIL_DOORS.length)`, `visible(id).length - visible(null).length === 1`), and keep a `toBeGreaterThan(0)` floor so an emptied list cannot pass by having nothing left to check. A fixed count is legitimate only where the number IS the ruling — the founder's one-word-per-door rule is asserted per label, never as a total. **Third shape of [[F-188]] in one day**: a guard coupled to what the code happened to look like rather than to what it must never do. |
| **F-190**| **An `undefined` field is OMITTED from a PostgREST write, so the column silently keeps its default — the reason every failed preview had no reason**| **FIXED 2026-09-04 by A2 (P-68).** `ci-poll-tick` wrote `failure_reason: result.ok ? null : result.reason` and `ChangesetDeployResult.reason` is `string \| undefined`. An explicit `null` writes NULL; an `undefined` is dropped from the JSON body entirely, so PostgREST never names the column and it keeps its default — **the same NULL, reached by a path no reader can tell apart from "nobody tried to set it".** The one failed deployment in production carries exactly that: `failure_reason` NULL on a row whose reason WAS computed, one line above, and thrown away. Its visible cost was two surfaces: the Ship hold card could only say *"nothing on the attempt says why"*, and the retry backoff — which spaces itself off the newest recorded attempt — had nothing to read. **This is the ledger's own "a column no writer sets" shape with a writer that thinks it is setting it**, which is worse: the code reads as correct, the type is honest (`?: string`), and only the wire format reveals it. **The rule: on a write, an optional field must be coalesced at the call site (`?? <a true sentence>`), never passed through as possibly-undefined** — and a "we do not know" case deserves words rather than an empty column, because a NULL will be read as "not attempted". Sibling of [[F-188]] and [[F-189]] in one respect: the defect is invisible in the file and visible only in what the system does with it. |
| **F-191**| **A guard whose SUBJECT is defined by where a file happens to end: `src.slice(src.indexOf("export const thing"))` runs to EOF, so the next function appended below it silently joins the thing under test**| **FIXED 2026-09-04 by A2 (P-71b), five sites.** Appending one server function to `track.functions.ts` broke two guards that had nothing to do with it: `asking-before-the-promise…` asserts `checkForecastObservable` "takes NO decisionId" and `parked-work-must-be-visible` asserts `getParkedWork` "is a reader and writes nothing" — both slice from their function's `export const` **to the end of the file**, so the new function's `decisionId` and its `.update(` became theirs. Neither failure described a defect; both named the wrong function. **Two more in the same family, found the same night:** a pairing regex requiring `\n` between two keys that prettier had joined onto one line, and a count asserting exactly nine clears directly beneath a comment promising "a tenth added tomorrow is covered the day it lands". **And I wrote a fifth into the new guard while fixing the other four** — bounded the slice on the string `"Out of budget"`, which the comment-stripper had already removed, so `indexOf` returned **-1**, the slice ran to EOF anyway, and it found `attempts:` in an unrelated hold. That is the sharpest version: `indexOf` returning -1 does not fail, it **silently widens the subject to the whole file**, so the guard keeps passing or fails for a reason that has nothing to do with its name. **The rule: bound every slice at both ends (`indexOf(next export, from)`), assert BOTH ends were found (`expect(from).toBeGreaterThan(-1)`), and never anchor on text a preprocessing step removes.** Third member of [[F-188]] and [[F-189]]'s family in one night: a guard coupled to the shape of the file rather than to the behaviour it names. |
| **F-192**| **`tsc` cannot check a PostgREST relation name, so a read of a table that does not exist typechecks, throws at runtime, and is indistinguishable from an empty result**| **GUARDED 2026-09-04 by A2 (after P-59c).** `whyShipStopped` read `.from("changesets")`; the table is `studio_changesets`, as four other reads in the SAME FILE already said. PostgREST answered 42P01, the throw set `isError`, and the Ship hold card fell back to a generic sentence while the record underneath was perfectly intact. **It shipped in P-59 and survived P-68 and P-68b, because all three verified the code and not the served screen** — A1 found it by reading the product. This is [[F-25]]'s dead-COLUMN class one level up. The guard is `every-from-names-a-real-table.test.ts`: every string literal passed to `.from()` in non-test `src` must be a key of `Tables` or `Views` in the generated `types.ts`, which is regenerated from the live schema. **It found two more the first time it ran.** `schema_migrations` is a Supabase system relation outside `public` (benign, listed with its reason). **`user_ai_rate_limits` does not exist in any schema at all** — `checkUserAiRateLimit` reads it, the read answers 42P01, and its `catch` logs a warning and returns `{ allowed: true }`, so **per-user AI rate limiting has never blocked a single request in this product's life**; it fails open on a table nobody ever created, and its own comment ("budget caps remain the hard gate") reads as a deliberate degradation rather than the total absence it is. Needs a founder call — create the table, or delete the limiter — and is recorded in the guard's exception list with that reason rather than hidden. **What the guard cannot catch, stated rather than implied: a name built at runtime, and a relation that exists in the database but not yet in regenerated types.** |
| **F-193**| **A real account's own workspace was silently unreadable since 2026-07-22 — an owner with no `workspace_members` row, and no surface could tell them why**| **FIXED 2026-09-04 by A3 (P-65).** Checked live before writing the migration: two workspaces existed with an owner and no matching member row — the probe workspace (created by SQL) and, separately, **"My Workspace" (`is_sample: false`), a genuine account's own workspace, broken the same way since creation.** `is_workspace_member()` gates read access everywhere, so this owner could not see their own workspace on any surface, with no error naming why — `resolveStartWorkspace`'s own refusal was machine copy (`error-copy.ts`'s `MACHINE` list drops "forbidden"), so even the one place that threw said nothing a person could act on. Fixed at the root: a safety-net trigger on `workspaces` (`AFTER INSERT`, `ON CONFLICT (workspace_id, user_id) DO NOTHING`, migration `20260909090000`) guarantees the owner's member row for every future insert, and the same migration backfilled both existing orphans. **The rule: an ownership relationship enforced by application code in one place (`createWorkspace`'s own explicit insert) and never guaranteed at the schema level will eventually be reached by a code path that forgot it** — `ensure_user_default_workspace`'s SECURITY DEFINER path always wrote the row; a second, later path (direct SQL, or any future insert) had no reason to know that was required. |
| **F-194**| **Four packets refused a decision AFTER a seat had made it; the fix was to ask the person BEFORE the dispatch**| **CLOSED 2026-09-04 by A2 (P-71 → P-71e).** R-39 says a call on the person's sentence alone is theirs. The first four attempts all sat downstream of a seat that had already decided, and each was defeated by a shape the last one had not imagined. **P-71** refused a `do-not-build` whose rationale rested on absence — the seat then declined on a track whose footing had been overwritten by a continuation. **P-71c** made the footing durable — the seat then declined again, correctly caught, and the sweep drove the track anyway. **P-71d** kept the track out of the sweep and refused either answer *while the Choice stood* — and on a FRESH sentence the seat recorded a **build**, approved, with a forecast it wrote, on the FIRST pass, before any Choice existed to stand. Four fixes, each correct, none finishing it. **The shape they shared: they all asked "may this decision be recorded?" when the money had already been spent and a seat had already formed an opinion.** P-71e asks the person before the crew is dispatched at all — on a carried track arriving at Decide there is nothing to weigh, because Sense has already reported the workspace holds nothing bearing on the sentence, so dispatching a strategist spends money to have it invent a forecast. **The rule: when a decision is not ours to make, the place to say so is before the work that makes it, not at the row it writes.** A refusal at the write is a last line and reads like a first one; every path that reaches it has already cost something and already has an answer it wants to record. Sibling of the prose-rule class in [[F-188]]: enforcement placed where it is *checkable* rather than where it is *effective*. |
| **F-195**| **A station's exit asked whether it FILED something, never whether its work was DONE — and exactly three stations can differ**| **CLOSED 2026-09-04 by A2 (P-123, `cae865835`), and the argument terminates rather than opening a sweep.** Twice in twenty minutes the tablet track left Ship without shipping: at 06:30:48 on a release-verifier's DECLINED decision (*"Do not ship until PRD approved and design gate cleared"*), and at 06:51:17 with the promote decision raised and unanswered. Both passed every gate on the advance, because **every gate asks about an ARTIFACT and a decision is an artifact** — `producedThisVisit` asks whether the station filed anything, `STATION_NEEDS.learn` asks whether a spec, changeset or deployment exists.
